[OR BABY' A SKIN

SCALP AND HAIR

Something for Mothers to Think About EVERY CHILD born into the world with an inherited or early developed tendency to distressing, disfiguring humours of the skin, scalp, and blood, becomes an object of the most tender solicitude, not only because of its suf- fering but because of the dreadful fear that the disfigura- tion is to be lifelong and mar its future happiness and prosperity. Hence it becomes the duty of mothers of such afflicted children to acquaint themselves with the best, the purest, and most effective treatment available, viz., THE CUTICURA TREATMENT.

ATHLETICS.

Cricket

Local cricket was again interfered with by rain on Saturday, and several matches had to be abandoned. The Warrington first eleven met Earlestown at Arpley, but after about an hour's cricket there was such a heavy downpour of rain, that the pitch was rendered unfit for further play and stumps were drawn at six o'clock. The visitors had knocked up 77 for the loss of seven wickets, Alderson (21 not out), Miller (15) and Husband (10) being the top scorers. The bowling of Podmore was responsible for three wicketsl at a cost of 28 runs, whilst Lynes took 2 for 29, and Connor 1 for 1.

Warrington second still hold an unbea47n certificate, though they came as near losing it on Saturday as they are likely to do in any other match during the season. They paid a visit to Earlestown and got the homosters out for 103. When their turn came to bat Warrington bad an anxious time, but thanks to 10 from the last man, Bennett, and the 10 extras they succeeded in top- ping Earlestown's score by one run. R Pierpoint With 21 was top scorer for the winners, and J F Reade, 26, for the losers.

Despite the unfavourable weather Warrington Gram- mar School and Fairfield played their match to a finish. The school eleven had first knock and declared their ianings after compiling 52 for the loss of 9 wickets. Mainly owing to the splendid batting of Boardman, who rattled up 35 not out, Fairfield were enabled to top this total by a single run,for the loss of only 5 wickets. Ball (12) also did good service with the bat.

Sankey Wire Mills 2nd visited Appleton and were beaten by the Stratton and Appleton eleven by a margin of 2E3 runs. J G Hatton (22) batted in fine' style for the victors, whilst Clegg and Cook (11 each) were the Wire Mills m ost successful batsmen. Moorfield for Appleton bowled with deadly effect, capturing six wickets for 18 runs. Foot Racing. At tbe Wigan County Police Sport., held at Spring- field Park on Saturday, S D Parsonage, a member of the Sankey Harriers, won the one mile handicap in the good time of 4min. 20 4-ssec.

1 Coronatidn Service at Nycliffe I Church. Sermon on "God Save the King." A large congregation assembled in Wycliffe Congre- gational Church, Beweey-street, Warrington, on Sunday evening, at a special service held in anticipation of the Coronation of the King. A number of bannerettes were displayed in the organ charaber,one of them bearine the 1 ejaculation" God save the King" and another " Long may he reign." Appropriate aetriotic hymns and I anthems were sung and the organist (Mr W H Payton, I Mies Bac), played special selections on the organ. The I introductory voluntary was "Coronation March " by P Godfrey, Mus Sac, and at the cl ose of the dervice Handel'a "Hallelujah " (Messiah) was played, the con- gregation standing meanwhile. The hymns included one by T H Gill" Lift thy song among the nations," and another, specially written by our townsman, Alfred Carney, entitled " Coronation Hymn." This last named hymn has been wedded to beautiful music by Mr W H Payton, Mus Sac, anti the singing of it by the congregation was a distinct feature of the service. The psalm " The earth is the Lord's " (Barriby in E) and the "To Daum" in F (Henry Smart) were also rung. The choir gave a credit- able interpretation of the anthem " The King shall rejoice," from " Judas Maccabeus " (Handel). Before the pronouncing of the benediction, the Natioaal Anthem was heartily joined in by the congregation, the ' third verse being sung as a solo by Mrs R El Pearson. The offertory was on behalf of the deficiency fuud of the London Missionary Society. , An appropriate sermon was delivered by the Rev Jobe I Yonge. The preacher based his discourse on the text, I " God save the King "(I Samuel 1.0 c., part of 24 v.) That was an expression, he said, which would be in universal use just now. Multitudes would be shouting it in loud acclaim all over the empire, very largely pro- bably with only superficial idea of its real significance. In effect it was a devout desire for the State. " Let it be remembered and realised," the preacher continued, " at this interesting period of our national history that ' the crown and sceptre of England, Scotland. Ireland, India and the British Colonies are iocaleulably greater than any personage who 'may be entrusted with them. The crown and the soeptre are the synsbols, the insignia of the Sovereign State. The Sovereign State is the vast , unity of a free people. The British monarchy lis not peewee and absolute—and never has I been so lawfully since the days of Magna ICharts. It is constitutional, and eermissive and repre- sentative. We know nothing and believe less of any " Divine right of Kings " By common consent of his eo-called subjects our Monarch reigns but he does I not rule. In this kingdom it is the people who are sovereign. That is a fundamental principle of the con- stitution and the day is assuredly zoming when this sovereignty of the people will find realisation through- out the widespread units of this whole empire. One of highest glories of the Britieh Crown, specially since the days of the Stuarts, has been the unfettered liberty of its people. The peculiar excellence and patriotism of the late lamented Qaeen was ever seen in her absolute loyalty to this fundamental principle ,of the consitution, There is every reason to believe that the present King will, in that respect, not be found wanting." The preacher proceeded to point oit that it was impossible. to lose sight of the personality of the King. Many loya_ intercessions would doubtless be offered in his behalf Not only for his own sake, but for the sake of his vener ated mother who lives in universal and grateful memory and of the noble stainless father, whom only the elders can recall. King lward would be kindly thought of on his Coronation day. It could not be denied that be succeeded to a difficult initeritanoe. There was an ideal attaching to the British Crown to- day such as it never possessed in the ages before Victoria. The position of Monarch of this Realm—with all its history, with all its enlightenment, with all its ,1 Christian light and life and liberty—had no superior I upon the face of the globe ; and the man who was called to occupy it could alone rightly discharge its functions and responsibilities by laying aside every- thing that could dim ite splendour, mar its honour and jeoperdise its Christian name. In referring to the Coronation service, the preacher said it served to set forth that even the Sovereign derived hia rank, his duty his privilege and his responsibility from the "King of , kings." His (the preacher's) feeling was that this great event should he eelebrated most religiously, not only in thelbistoric abbey but throughout the kingdom. Why should not the first public act in this town next Thurs- day morning, he asked, have been a great united repre- sentative service. It had been possible of arrangement in many places, why should it be found impossible here or anywhere ? It ought not to be impossible here or any- where, and that it proved so, was too frequently an evidence of the unhappy supremacy of sectarian ex- clusiveness over the broad and liberal spirit of the Gospel. Some day all this religious narrowness would be gloriously overcome. It could not last for ever, even on earth, and it had not existence in leaven ! The ' moment that inglicans and Romans and Nonconformists gained the other side of the River, they would shake off the ignoranees and prejudices of this imperfect, lower life as they clasped inseparable hands, and their sur- prise would be everwhelming that ever fellow be- lievers in the same " Lord over all " could have lived all through the earthly course in alienatioe and differ- ence and distance. Continuing, the preacher pointed out that the King bad a magnificent opportunity, if he would carry as King into his remaining years the motto " I serve," which specially belonged to him as Prince of Wales—Edward VII would be a sovereign worthy of both his succession and of his kingdom. Reference was made to the open letter of the Social Democratic Federation, which the preacher trusted the King would see and ponder over. The letter conteined counsels to which the King might wisely listen. They Pointed to a sound and patriotic police. They marked Gut a new departure which the King had peculiar "Pacity as well as great opportunity to inaugurate. Els Pursuit might entail much devotion of labour and of time, curtailing possibly the leisure for some things which the first person in the realm would do more wisely to es.chew, but the welfare of the neion and of the empire was the supreme concern of the King, and there was ample reason to believe that he was conscious of its being so. Coming to the throne in his elder years, his servictehcaotuhldis onlym imind a mbauyt b elimited.gu iguided alibi; time for kinw gl eyll pray of all wisdom, and his heart impelled tpheeoplespmiraity by the spirit of truth and love, so as to render the fullest service, that might yet be possible wycl iffeprePsi.dSed.Aa.t tthe Society.meeting afternoon, when there was a good outset, Mr Owen referred attendance. Ile proceeded to speak on the value of truthfulness for raising a man in the estimation of his fellowe.—The speaker for the after- noon gave an address on "What is the best life to live ?" He contrasted a self-centred life with that of Christ'and showed that a life which gave most was highest. me w H Wallington w ar4 the eoloibte and his services were greatly appreciated. AMONG THE KING'S SUBJECTS IN

POVERTY LAND.

"Is it a fact," said one Strand-street hawker to another, " that Soapy Joe is invited to the King's dinner ? " The other nodded. The first speaker shrugged his shoulders. " I did think they'd dror the line somewhere," he remarked, in a tone of supreme contempt. " Soapy Joe's no clarss—he eats winkles!" And, in his scorn, the fighting cocks he was vending were nearly overbalanced, as he mused on the democracy of the age. "I won't deny," he went on in a judieial manner, having eold some wares in the interim, which caused him to take a more genial view of things in general—" I won't deny but that Joe 'as 'is good point& But morals before manners, see I, and pertickler on an occasion of this sort. If Joe was ensiled on 'is Gospel honour if 'espaid 'is washer-woman 'is three-'alf pence a week reglar for 'is linen 'e would 'ave to own up it's many a daiy since she 'as seen the colour of his money. But as to me, I pays up punctual—'cept when I'm in quod. Yet Joe's to get 'is mug an' not me. Old Bill the Grumbler aired his grievanee to several pals during the day, hut they were one and all too polite to tell him that His Majesty's guests were selected not from their fastidiousness with regard to everyday viands, but from the rarity with which they were the compulsory guests of Government. Bill's lauedrese might give him a very good character: the magistrate would certainly give him a very bad one. Street hawkers are nomads by nature ; the general- ity of them have no home, no belongings, no ambitions beyond their daily wants. Their little tray of toy!, cheap jewellery,:or boot laces,and the clothes they stand in,ooestitute their whole earthly inapedimenta.When they have sold sufficient for their immediate needs, they slink away down a side street,like moles glad to burrow back into darkness ; they orawl into some dingy:eating-house or fifth-rate " pub," and exchange their greasy coppers for a modicum of liquor. Then drawing out their pipe and filling it with the rankest of tobacco, they smoke, and are as happy as they ever can be here below. They are a silent lot,very cautious about making new "pals," very suspicious of a strange face, and eaten up with small jealousies of each other. When Morpheise calls, they slouch out of the deor,with never a farewell to their companions,and shamble along till they reach a dreary pile of brick with" Fourponee a bed " engraved in white or gold letters, on the gas-lamp burning dimly in the doorway. Here they pay their money, receive a number, and, passing the turnstile, enter the general hall—a chamber often in the basement, in winter warm and stuffy, in summer (dose and over-powering. Papers lie strewn en the tables, dominoes and draughts are scattered on the bare forms; the flaring, unshaded gas-borners throw light on groups of men mostly in their shirt-sleeves, with ears and w ant deeply engraved on their haggard, grimy faces, but seldom diseontent, and never despair. On the contrary, they are more cheerful inside than they were out. They have cheated the workhouse again for one mere day; they leave paid for their bed, and to- morrow may take care of itself. The whining tone, the cadging voilee are dropped ; taciturnity gives way to joeosencss, and grimness to mirth ; greetings are ex- changed, hut not confidences—for the strange thing is that,in spite of the homelessness, the absence of personal property, the solitariness of the life, these people are never alone. Day and night they are watched and sus- pected,by the proprietor of their "hotel," by the police, by each other. Every friend may be an enemy in dis- guise, and if two'' coves "are seen to fraternise they immediately become the bugbear of the whole commun- ity, and to every action is given a sinister motive. Se, dissembling is in the very air they breathe, and the most ingenious liar is the one who will lead the life of greatest peace. While collectively they can do generous actions, such as penny colleetions for a pal " stony broke," or bizeing an ounce of tabacco for one " down on his luck," in- dividual charity is looked upon as tbs mereet clap-trap sentiment, and excites but derision ihd contempt. An inmate of one of these estaislishmente, who had been chummy with a fellow-boarder for some seven years, was asked by the latter to lend him a coat, as the pleader had not even fourpenee for a bed, and had to walk the streets on a bitter winter night. The coat was lent, and the borrower never appeared again. The lender was jeered at for his henevolence,and was known I as " Greenhorn "for many a long day after. 33 little trust is reposed in his fellow-creature, that each man sleeps with his coat as a pillow, and pawns what extra clothing he may possess, for even a locker may be picked in hislablenee and all articles appropri- ated, Strikes are not uncommon in the rougher sett of " focirpenny dosses." The young bloods are unruly,and the manager is often drawn from as rough a class as themselves. Hence a frequent fracas, which may or may not end at the police-station. It is a strict rule that lodgers should not introduce their performing bears, their screaming macaws, etc. Ow sue ,fgaseian an itaiian managed to eeerete his monkey beneath his coat, and to take it with him to his bunk. Someone " split," and the manager ordered hie ejection. This was easier said than dine, for the dor- mitories rose as one man in the Italian's defence ; crockery Sew, beads were broken, and the battle raged for four hours before they discovered that the Italian, horror-struck at the pandemonium he had raised, had slipped away, taking the monkey with him. The result was summonses in the police-court ; but the rioters had their revenge later. The manager happened to be going to a wedding, sad brought his best clothes to the office, meaning to change there. These were seen and purloined, pawned, and the pawaticket sent him anthymousiy, though the money thus obtained was spent in a roaring good feed at his expense. Be never discovered the players of this practical joke, which reminds one very much of the pranks of University undergraluates. To look at the ordinary specimen ofeinwn-trodden humanity, wise hawke his penny toys so meekly and se assioloously in the City street', one would hardly believe him capable of such lankiness. But, uniting vary much diseased,:they all possess a curious buoyancy which is partly' date perisape, to utter freedom from resonsibility. They have never to think of rent, or children ; fashion imposes no style, seasons no extra buries. To keep out of the workhouse as long as they can is their one object, though I must own they have no idea of thrift. If they make but sixplence, they will live, or rather starve, on that sixpence ; if they make two shillings, it goes as quickly. Twopen'orth of gin will just keep them alive,ibut six twopen'orths is better and twelve is best of all. Some of them live on liquor I from one week's end to the other. Gin, varied with steucis the prevailing taste. They are never the worse 11 in the strict sense of the term, for the doses are small, I and they live in the open-air; but one can hardly fancy they are any the better. Chewing tobacco takes the place of food. Need I any that a taste if the King's 1 beef will be a feast mush appreciated, and is being looked forward to most eagerly ? Their makings are very precarious—from a shilling a day to fifteen shillings a week, but a rainy season spells bard luele—for whes stops to buy under an umbrella ? Dripping and :drenched, the disappointed vendor drags his aethicg form to the cheerless dots-house, to emerge again with a parcel destined for the pop-shop, and his musings that evening are gloomy indeed. An asthmatic ; cough, a twinge of rheumatism, or a touch of broil- ' shitis willlremind him that on no account whatever may one be ill in the doss-house. _ _

At the first symptom of a serious breakdown the manager asks no questions, listens to. no protest, but paeks the unfortunate off to the infirmary Aole z °lens at a moment's notice, not allowing the patient to send word even to distant relatives if he has any, which is more than doubtful. - . .

Therefore,we cannot wender if the poor street hawker isialmost heroic in his efforts at concealment of a malady. He stiles his cough, walks straight when twisted with' lumbago, and declares his " bloomin' carcase 'as never been better," though the same " bloomin' carcase 2' is racked with rheumatic pains. As long as he is lade- pendent a quota of happinese belongs to him, and he shows :this independence by his love of roaming. Sel- dom his night quarters know him long. Hs will change for a mere trifle. The bread is stale, the tea weak, the manager has bounced or a cove peached, and he tells you airily he has changed his " hotel," and the "luggage will follow presently." . . . .

Some of the hawkers who are enterprising contrive to eke out their income by odd jabs at night, by running for cabs, by selling " extry editions," by getting taken en as supers at a shilling a night, or,if they own a voice, by singing in the suburbs in hopes that a friendly " Area Belle " will be struck by their musical talent. Their favourite song is " Home, sweet home," thougit they do not see tho irony of its application to themselves. Some go strawberry-picking in the summer, also hop- picking has its votaries ; but year by year it is getting more difficult to be engaged by chance, the laws against vagrants are more strictly enforced, and the man who tramps it into Kent hoping for a remunerative holiday, may find himself committed as a vagrant before he can say" Jack Robinson." As Bill the Grumbler says sarcastically : " Unless we can drive down in our motor car and scatter gold to beggars 'long the way like a South African , millionaire, we're not wanted, and they tell us the same pretty plain." But some of them are going to be the King's guests, l and life just at present is worth living for that.— Cassell's Penny Magazine.

It is a lofty theme—Fels-Naptha soap, the money-back soap, that brings ease and rest and an open future to millions. 2,1(1 a bar.

THE PUBLIC AND PRIVATE LIFE OF

THE KING AND QUEEN.

COPYRIGHT.]

It was on November 9th, 1841, that the glad tidings of the birth of a son and heir to Queen Victoria were announced to the nation. The great event took place at Buckingham Palace, and from here the Prince Consort sent messen- gers to the Archbishop of Canterbury and the Lord President of the Council, but, owing to his anxiety for the Queen's health, not until after the birth of the little Prince, At two o'clock in the afternoon, however, all the Minksters of State were present at a Council, when the good news was announced to them, and they, in turn, ordered a general public thanksgiving. In the Royal household excitement reigned. Messengers were despatched to the Dowager Queen Adelaide at Marlborough House, to the Duchess of Cambridge at Kew, and to the Guild-. hall, where John Pine was just taking up office as Lord Mayor. At the City Banquet in the even- ing there was a scene of extraordinary enthusiasm, when the distinguished company sprang to their feet to respond to the toast of Her Majesty and the infant Prince. Some slight annoyance was felt by that section of the public who were jealous of the German influence of the Prince Contort when the heir ta the British throne was first gazetted by the title of Duke of Saxony; but these were appeased when he was created by Patent Prince of Wales and Earl of Chester. He also bore a long list of other titles, including those of Duke of Saxony, Duke of Cornwall and Rothesay, Earl of Carrick, Baron of Renfrew, Lord of the Isles, Great Steward of Scotland, and eight years later Earl of Dublin. This infant of many titles was introduced into a nursery of imposing status. The head nurse, Mrs. Brough, was paid a salary of £l,OOO, and Mrs. Hull, whom the King still fondly remem- bers as "dear old May," was of hardly lees importance. Some amusing complications arose on account of the hour and the date of the Prince's birth. It was the custom by precedent to give sundry rewards and titles on the birth of an heir to the throne. Among these, it was usual to give a major's commission to the officer on guard at the Royal palace; but it happened that the child was born at 10-48, the exact moment when the guard was being changed, and it was difficult to deter- mine whether the officer of the guard just going off or of that just coming ou should receive the honour.

Then, again, it was the custom for the Mayor of Chester to be given a baronetcy upon the birth of the Heir-apparent, as the infant is always created Earl of Cheater. But in the present case the heir being born on "Lord Mayor's Day," when all the mayors in the kingdom are changed, it was open to lively discussion whether the banker just relinquishing office or the linen- draper just accepting it should have a right be the privilege. The baptism ef tho Royal heir took place in St. George's Chapel, Windsor, on January 25th, 1842. It was delayed so long in order that King Frederick William IV. of Prussia, who de- sired to stead sponsor to the infant, might be present at the ceremony. The Prince's other sponsors were Prince George, Duke of Cambridge, the Duchess of Cambridge, on behalf of the Duchess of Saxe-Coburg, and Princess Augusta of Cambridge—on behalf of Princess Sophia. The little Prince received the names Albert Edward, exceptionally few compared with those of most royal children. After the service a magnificent banquet was held in the State dining-room of Windsor Castle, and the Queen afterwards gave a reception in the Waterloo Chamber, where the gigantic christening cake, more than eight feet in circumference, was duly cut up and distributed among the guests. This christening ranks in magnificence with the most splendid festivities in history, the expenses of that one day's entertainment being reckoned at two hundred thousand pounds. The child was now very properly relegated to the obscurity and seclusion of the nursery, and grew into a fine healthy boy, with high spirits and remarkable intelligence, though he was very ohy in company. He was brought up with the Simplicity in4hich Queon,Victoria knew was the right training for her children. Like any other British child of gentle parents, the boy nestled in his mother's lap and listened to her words of good counsel and to her stories of brave men and true, and with his curly head pressed against the bosom of the mother of a great people he learnt the secret of that love which wins the hearts of all men born of women.

Queen Victoria was,- however, severe in her discipline. The first duty of those who have to rule is to learn to obey. The Queen compelled obedience from her children, not only to herself but to those appointed for their guidance, and any lapse in this respect was always followed by speedy punishment. One day when Male Albert Edward displayed his high spirits by an act of childish wilfulness he was promptly picked up by the Queen Mother and elippered there and then before the entire Court! The Prince's early education was entrusted to his tutors, first to the Rev. M. Birch, rector of Prestwich, and then to Mr. Thomas Gibbs (knighted afterwards for his services). He spent a considerable time at Osborne in the Isle of Wight, and here with his brothers and sisters he was taught manual labour in the garden and field, and with spade and trowel. Two or three hours each day he and his brothers worked side by side with ordinary workingmen, under the direction of a foreman who criticised their work and sent in a weekly report to the Prince Con- qort, who paid his sons their wages according to the amount of work done. Very early in life the Prince obtained the education of travel. As a small child he visited Scotland and Ireland, and a little later went with his father and mother to Paris, where they stayed with Napoleon 111. and Empress Eug4ne. So delighted was he with the good time he was having—so different to the restraints and simplicity of his own home—that with childish candour he asked whether he might stay longer. The Empress told him with a smile that his parents could not spare him, at which he re- marked that he was sure he would not be missed, "as there were plenty more of them in the nursery at home." When he was seventeen the Prince took up, residence at the White Lodge, Richmond, with hie tutor, Mr. Gibbs, by whom he was prepared for his confirmation, which took place in April of 1858 in St. George's Chapel, Windsor. The Archbishop of Canterbury officiated on this occa- sion, and after the ceremony the Prince held a reception and received many splendid gifts and the congratulations of his family and friends. It was in November of this year that the Prince received the precious letter from Queen Victoria in which Her Majesty wrote to him in touching terms and informed him that he must now consider himself free from parental control. In words of melting tenderness she bade him remember the love that she and the Prince Con- sort had shown towards him, explaining to his that, though he might have been tempted to regard his upbringing as unduly strict, their only desire had been to strengthen him to stand against the temptations that beset the life of princes. So profoundly was the Prince moved by this Queenly and motherly eloquence that upon reading it he burst into tears, and again when showing it to his friend, Gerald Wellesley.. Upon arriving at a suitable age the Prince studied for a session at Edinburgh, and after- wards entered Christ Church, Oxford, where he listened to the lectures of Charles Kingsley and ' others, and was under the discipline of Dr. Whewell, old "Billy Whistle" as he was affec- tionately termed. He stayed at Oxford for a year only, and afterivards resided for three or four terms at Cambridge. His Majesty looks back on those old undergraduate days with affec- tion. He made many a good friend and joined in many a college "spree" with a lack of all priggishness that made him a general favourite. In the summer of 1860 the Prince went on a tour through the United States and Canada, where he was received with boundless en- thusiasm. The handsome lad, so full of spirit and life, eager to see the wonders of the New World, with a great capacity for enjoyment, and an overflowing sympathy, the ‘heir to the great motherland to which Canada was still proud to own herself a part, and in which the ITnitel States recognised her dearest historical associa. tions and the closest ties of blood, captivated the hearts of every man and maid on the other side of the water. In December, 1861, the Prinie stiffered a deep loss by the death of his father, This noble

gentleman had maintained a profound influence ever his son, who looked up to him with respect and admiration. The King haa always kept sacred .the anniversary of the Prince Consort's death, retiring into seclusion and privacy, even when on his triumphal progress through India In 1875, and when travelling elsewhere. When it,was decided to erect the mausoleum at Frog- niore in honour of his father's memory, the Prince was the first to come forward and sub- scribe £lO,OOO out of his own purse.

In the following year His Royal Highness, accompanied by Dean Stanley, travelled in the East, visiting those places in the Holy Land which have for ever been sanctified by Christ's life and death.

Queen Victoria was now seriously considering matrimonial projects for her son. It had always been decided between her and her late Consort that the heir to the British Crown should wed a German Princess. But " marriages are made in Heaven," and in the present case a curious accident intervened which directed His Royal Highness's 4ffections in another quarter. The story reads- like a page out of The Arabian Nights, but it is true nevertheless. The Prince was conversing one day with a young friend whose rank was sufficient to permit of some intimacy when he desired to show the Prince a miniature of his fiancee. He handed a small portrait to our present King, but he had made a mistake, for it was not the portrait of the lady he had mentioned, but one of Alexandra, Princess of Denmark. The Prince was imme- diately fascinated by the charming face repre- sented, and having made inquiries as to who she was, he said with a sudden burst of ardour, "'That lady shall be my wife." It happened that shortly afterwards—not wholly by accident—Alexandra of Denmark and Albert Edward of England both visited the Cathedral City of Worms. Those in the Prince's suite were somewhat surprised at the enthusiasm with which he studied the frescoes of that cathe- dral, but one or two most closely intimate with him were allowed to understand that the charm- ing vivacious beauty of a young seventeen-year- old Princess was of more interest to him than medinval mural paintings. Upon his return to England the Prince in- formed his Queen-Mother that he desired to marry this young lady, and with all deference to his mother's wishes, he would marry none other. Her late Majesty was surprised and not altogether ready to acquiesce in an arrange- ment so entirely original on the part of her son. However, seeing that he was intensely serious, the Queen journeyed to Denmark to judge for herself whether her son's choice was a suitable one. Her Majesty's doubtfulness was soon dis- pelled, and she, too, was charmed with the delightful simplicity, vivacity, and beauty .of the Danish Princess.

_ Alexandra Caroline Marie Charlotte Louise Julie is the second daughter of -Prince and Prineess Christian of Schleswig-Holstein, who became King and Queen of Denmark shortly after the marriage of their first daughter, Dagmar, whose husband was afterwards Czar of Russia. On her father's side she is descended through eleven generations from. Christian 1., Who was elected King of Denmark in 1443, and successively became King of Norway, King of Sweden and Duke of Schleswig-Holstein. On her mother's she is allied to the German Royal House. \ The life of the Princess in her Danish home was extremely simple. The household was conducted on rules of the strictest economy, for Prince Christian was poor, and could indulge in no ex- travagances. Alexandra and her brothers and sisters were brought up on strictly domestic lines, and the girls had to sew and take their share in all household duties. Nor was their education neglected. "I had to learn," said the Princess once; "we were always taught that it was necessary." From this home the Princess was invited by Queen Victoria to stay with Her Majesty at Windsor, and during this visit the Prince pro- ceeded with his wooing ardently, and before his sweetheart returned to Denmark arranged a definite date for the marriage. This took place on the 10th of March, 1863, at St. George's Chapel, Windsor, and was the occa- sion of great national rejoicing and enthusiasm. The British people were struck vith admiration at the fresh beauty and sweetness of the young bride, and the Prince himself glowing with pride, made the most tender bridegroom. On that great day he said with sincere emotion, "I assure you, I know now what it is to feel happy. If I can make the future life and home of the Princess a happy one. I shall be content." At the great re- ception held at St. James's Palace on March 20th, to present the Princess tn the Court, the Prince, turning tn a lady, whispered enthusiastically, "Is she not a pet? Is she not a darling?" Marlborough House was set apart as the London residence of the Royal pair, and the Prince spent at least 1350,000 in furnishing it and arranging it according to his wife's taste and convenience. This mansion, erected by Sir Christopher Wren, became the official London house of the Royal Family after the Prince's marriage, and from time to time he made many alterations and improvements. There are now over one hundred rooms available, but even this number was not too many to accommodate a household of more than one hundred persons. As the Prince's children came into the world and grew up they all had their separate suite of apartments, and rooms had to be reserved for their tutors, governesses and attendants. Before the King's accession his London life at Marlborough House was singularly free from ceremony. A Scotch gillie in national costume adniitted visitors to the entrance hall, and by him were handed on to a page in simple dark blue coat and black trousers, who led the way to :he ante-room connected with the Prinee's private sitting-room. Here the Prince conducted his morning's business, with the assistance of Sir Dighton Probyn and Sir Francis Knollys, while " Vennie," the Irish terrier, and "Peter," a French bull dog, snored in unison on the hearth- rug. Much hard work was done in this sanctum, for with his great variety of interests, the busi- ness in connection with his great estates, his social duties, and private correspondence, there was always enough to occupy a full morning. At midday the Prince's brougham would come round; and he would pay visits before the lun- Aeon at 2-30. During the summer afternoons the Prince and Princess delighted to receive friends in the gardens. Here tea would be served in the rustic summer house or in striped tents furnished with Oriental rugs and garden seats. _

The "Indian Room" at Marlborough House is crowded with magnificent mementoes of the Prince's wonderful tour in India, containing among other treasures, a shield worth £20,000, and countless boxes, dishes, drink-vessels, trays, swords, scabbards, shields, idols, and ivory carvings of superb workmanship. The drawing- room is a spacious apartment supported by pillars of white and gold, and of an evening, lighted by countless mirrors reflecting the electric light with a dazzling and beautiful effect. The Prince and Princess gave many splendid banquets and balls at Marlborough House, but they much preferred the quieter and more healthy life in the country to which they retired on every possible occasion. It was Sandringham in Norfolk which claimed the greatest affection of our King and Queen, and which they regarded as their dearest home. The Prince purchased the place shortly after his marriage from Mr. Spence Cooper at a price of £200,000. It was then a straggling unkempt estate of more than 7,000 acres, but the Prince quite transformed it, with well made roads, splendid paddocks and kennels, good coverts, gardens and well-planned grounds. He also built some model schools in which he and the Princess always took a keen interest, visiting them periodically to examine the scholars, or award prizes. There is also' a spacious dairy, in which the Princess and her daughters need to learn butter- making. The inhabitants of the village of Sand- ringham were lucky people, and in sickness or in health-Avers constantly looked after by "the family," as they were affectionately called. On winter nights there was often a dance at the house, to which all the tenants were invited, and the Royal personages from the Prince and Prin- cess downwards, mixed with them freely, taking an interest in their personal affairs, and vying , with each other in their efforts to amuse their guests. And when any of them were ill the Prin- cess and her daughters were quick to bring cheering words and good things, to sit at the bedside reading, and generally tending to their wants. The cricket and football fields were scenes of much good sport in which the King's sons joined heartily. "I am a bit of a farmer myself in a small way," said thn King once, and this was a modest way of expressing the management of his Norfolk estate. Every day while • at Sandringham, wet or fine, the Prince could be seen trudging about the fields, giving instructions about the crops, visit- ing the paddocks and stables, where he bred some of the most splendid animals in England. turn-

I ins; into the dog -kennels, or feesting some" or the I pet animals belonging to one or other of the family. Like any other Norfolk squire, the King can discuss the value of land, price of a good crop, the points Of a horse or deg, or the milking qualities of a Shorthorn. Now and again the King gave a ball at Sand- vingham, and this always opened with a quad- rille in which the King and Queen invariably took "tops and bottoms." It is said that the King is a most energetic partner, and as he him- self expressed it always 'dances to the tune." In his younger days no.raan in the country could dance a Highland fling better than he did, and later on he delighted in all old-fashioned country dances, and especially a romping dance called the "Triumph" in which he excelled. After the dance the .men adjourned to the billiard and smoking rooms, or to the skittle- alley. The King is passionately fond of bowls and skittles, and likes a game immediately before dinner and again just before bed. On Sunday, no sooner had midnight struck than he would take a friend with him to the bowling alley and indulge in an hour's fun. Bedtime at Sandringham is at no fixed hour, and it is often in the small hours of the morning before the King retires; but he is always up very early, and has got through a good deal of work before most of his guests have sbruggled out of bed. The Queen is as fond of Sandringham as the King, and has her own hobbies in dairy, garden, and field. She is wonderfully fond of all living things, and has many favourite horses, ;legs, and birds to which she always pays a morning visit. In her dressing-room she keeps a number of singing birds, and one or two specially tame ones are allowed to fly about at will between the room and the saloon. At one time a beauti- ful blue pigeon had this privilege. The Queen's boudoir at Sandringham is a reflection of her own character—full of tender- ness and charm and womanly sympathy. The prevailing tone is a delicate pink, but the *walls are crowded with sketches in pencil and water- colour, and choice engravings surrounded by brackets of lovely china. Everywhere the eye roves it meets photographs filling every vacant space, and standing in frames on little tables, odd corners, and shelves, which already hold books and music, needlework and correspondence from friends, as well as many a gift from humble folk, prized not so much for its value' or beauty but for the love with which it was given. Here the Queen receives her friends, and writes some of her large correspondence. She is an enthusiastic letter-writer. Thirty or forty letters from her own pen is no unusual daily occurrenee, and she often sends off a number of telegrams, while Miss Knollyn, her close attendant, often writes at her instructions as many as a hundred letters a day. Both the King and Queen have•made ideal parents, and their family duties have been their greatest source of pleasure. "The Princess," said the nurse, Mrs. Blackburn, "was in her glory if she could find time to run up to the nursery, put on a flannel apron, wash the children, and see them asleep in their little cots." The Prince also was a fond father, always eager for a romp with his little ones, and never tired of telling them stories on hie knee or play- ing with them in the fields. A familiar sight to the neighbours of their home at Sandringham was the whole family huddled together in c, wagonette, rumbling along the country .roads with a merry din of tongues and shouts of laughter. The Princess brought up her children with the same simplicity that has characterised the up-bringing of all the Royal Family, and taught them lessons of obedience, consideration for others, and self-denial. A good story is told of Princess Maud when quite a baby girl. She was about to put a piece of bread and butter in her mouth, when she noticed one of the ladies at the Court looking at her very hard, surprised at the size of the mouthful. The child held out the piece, generosity overcoming reluctance. "You can have it if you want it so very much," she said. A general laugh ensued, and the lady was about to disclaim all desire for theemorsel when the Princess of Wales whispered, "Take it; she must learn to be thoughtful." The Prince was always anxious that his children should neither be flattered nor coddled. He al- ways spoke of his daughters as "the little girls," and never permitted either the boys or girls to be addressed as "Your Royal Highness," but merely as Prince Eddy or Princess Louise. The only toys they were allowed to receive were from relations, and were but simple and inexpensive. As soon as his sons were old enough he decided that a training-ship would be the best place at education for them, avoiding the danger of flat- tery at a public school, and likely to turn out his sons as manly fellows, with a knowledge of men end the world, and able, as he himself desired and expressed, "to do something with their own hands." So the two young Princes entered the "Britannia" training ship, and by their father's orders submitted to exactly the same discipline,, had exactly the same kit, and exactly the same privileges, neither mom nor less, as the cadets with whom they studied, drilled and messed. It was an excellent experience for them, and the two young middies grew into fine sailor-boys, up to every lark possible. Many is the yarn told of their pranks, such as when they ornamented each other's nose with the pollen from the orange stamens of the Bermuda lilies. The islanders of ,Bermuda were drawn up to receive the sons of Itheir future King, and their astonishment was , , 'groat when they beheld them with brilliant alarmed beyond together with other cadets, had been tattooed on ti the nose with an anchor! The report was thelaltowlyni nogsesj al beyond Thismn ue measure urr ery , twoaa and des aprar uetarhda that his badhiari osg sownbasys, be- lieved for a considerable time, but at last the Prince of Wales got Ciao telegraphic communica- tion with his sons' vessel, and was greatly re- lieved to learn that he had been wrongly in- formed. . . . . . ..

The King has always been devoted to his daughters,•the Princesses Louise, Victoria and Maud, but the last is his favourite, and on ac- count of tom-boy character, is called " Harry." A proof of his affection for them is that he has permitted them to marry men of their choice. It was believed by the public that he would never sanction the marriage of Princess Louise to the Earl of Fife, but the public, for once, was mistaken. It is known, too, that he had great ambitions.for the Princess Maud, but when she came to him and announced her love for Prince Charles of Denmark, he put his ambitions on one side, and upheld the marriage. Prince Eddy, as the late Duke of Clarence was always called, was his father's hope and joy. He was sent to Cambridge, after which the Prince of Wales decided that he should enter the 10th Hussars, being a horseman of exceptional merit. One of our King's happiest days was when he took his eldest son to the House of Lords, to take the oath as a Peer of the Realm, and another was when Prince Eddy received from the Queen the Order of the Garter, this being the first time that the Heir Apparent to the Throne and his son have ever held the Order at the same time.

But, alas! the tragedy came of poor Prince Eddy's death, and the Prince of Wales, who had himself tenderly nursed his son during his ill- ness,: was left broken-hearted. Those who saw him during this time say his grief was terrible, and it has left an ineffaceable impression: One who knows the Ring personally writes: "The overwhelming sorrow of those days forged perhaps the greatest link of love between the King and Queen of England. For many weeks after that sad event the stricken mother would see no one but the Prince, and he, and he only, as a husband and father who shared her sorrow was able to console her."

_ The public life of the sing and Queen since the early days of their marriage need only be briefly outlined. In 1863 the Prince of Wales took his beat in the House of Lords under the title of the Duke of Cornwall. The following year the Prince and Princess visited Oxford. when the former had degrees conferred upon Lim. In April 1867 the Prince became President ef St. Bartholomew's HoSPital, in which insti- tution he has always taken the keenest interest. It was in this year that the Princess of Walee suffered from the severe attack of rheumatic fever which left her with a slight though hardly per- ceptible limp. In 18€8 the Royal pair visited Ireland, where they received an enthusiastic reception, the Irish with their gift-of eloquence straightway dubbing the Princess "the Rose of England." The fol- lowing year the Prince and Princess visited napoleon 111. at Compiegne, and afterwards journeyed to Berlin, Stockholm, Copenhagen and Egypt: - - - . . .

It the latter country they had many interest- ing adventures wandering over the deserts and vNitinh, the Pyramids. At the latter the Prince created a diversion for the amusement of his wife by sending up rockets which had a very weird and enchanting effect in the transparent darkness of an Eastern night. After this they journeyed down the Nile to Pedro, where the Princess visited the harem of

the Khedive. The next place to which the Lai party journeyed was Constantinople, where the Sultan received them with lavish magnificence. From there they visited the Crimea, end went over the battlefields of the recent war, and then returned home via Athens, where the Princess had the pleasure of meeting her brother, King George. In 1871 the Prince was seized with the illness which so nearly proved fatal. It was an attack of typhoid fever, and for some weeks his life was despaired of. On February 27, 1872, he was suffi- ciently recovered to take part in the great Thanksgiving Service at Westminster Abbey, where all eyes were upon him, and hearty, thanks offered up for his recovery. The devotion of the Princess of Wales to her husband was seen at the conclusion of the ser- vice, when the Prince rose from his chair, looking terribly pale and weak, to take his part in the procession. Putting all ceremony aside the Princess hastened to her husband's side, and drawing his arm through hers led him gently down the steps of the chancel and along the nave of the Abbey. Countless small services as these, done tenderly and spontaneously. testify to the mutual affection existing between the King and Queen. In 1874 the Prince was elected Grand Master of the Freemasons in England, in succession to the Marquis of Ripon. On May sth, 1875, he was installed at the Freemasons' Hall as First Principal of the Royal Arch Freemasons In 1375-6 his Royal Highness visited India, and Anglo-Indians still look back upon that time of festivity and remember the almost miracu- lously gorgeous receptions of the native Princes,. when all the wealth of the Eastern Smpire seemed to be lavished upon rendering the Prince a magnificent homage. The Paris Exhibiticn of 1878 owed a good deal of its success to the presence and interest of his Royal Highness. In 1883 he attended the "silver wedding" of the Crown Prince of Germany with his eldest sister the Princess Royal of England, and he was then nominated Field Marshal of the German Army. In isas the Prince and Princess again visited Ireland, and in 1889. went to the new Paris Exhibition.

In 1892 their Royal Highnesses ruK:4;rthe irreparable loss of their eldest son, as mentioned above. A Royal Family, however, is not allowed to indulge in grief to the same extent as in humbler spheres. The following year their second son, Prince George, married the beautiful Princess May of Teck, and the children with whom they have since been blessed have been a. source of joy to their grandparents,

Whenever the Prince aid Princess of Wales are near the King, either in London or Sandring— ham, he never lets a day pass without visiting them and the bonny children, who make the tursery a merry place. little Prince Edward is his grandfather's pet, and the King is always eager to give him and the other little otres any treat permitted by their parents. And now once more came grief, and this time it was with the passing of the great Queen. The nation mourned for as good a sovereign as ever sat upon the English throne, mourned for her with a sorrow that has not yet passed, nor will do so as long as the gracious presence of Victoria is remembered by her people, whom she loved so well. The King's grief was greater than ours, ,for he was her son as well as subject,. and in losing a queen he lost a mother who ,had been his consoler in time of affliction, hiEr counsellor in time of difficulty, and also was the object of his reverent love. During .the long hours when our late and dear Sovereign lay breathing out her life Edward VII, watched and prayed, and when by the doctors' orders he was bound to leave her bedside, it was only te pace up and down the corridors waiting for news. When at last all was over he broke down quite, and weeping for ms,ny hours like a little child could not be comforted.

His proclamations to his people after his accession show the sincerity of his love for the late Queen, and not a sign of self-thought or pride in his new dignity. He thanked his people in every part of the Empire for their expressiou of sorrow and respect for Victoria, and he gauged their one desire when he repeated in varying words and phrases the sentiment of his first pro- clamation after the funeral: "I shall earnestly strive to walk in her footsteps, devoting myself to the utmost of my powers to maintaining and promoting the highest interests of my people, and to the diligent and zealous fulfilment of the great and sacred responsibilities which, throUgh the will of God, I am now called to undertake."

During the time that has passed between King Edward's Accession and Coronation, he has man- fully fulfilled the determination expressed above, and has maintained the high traditions of the, British Crown. He snared his son to make the great tour of the Empire. and has seen with in- finite satisfaction the loyal enthusiasm with. which the Heir Apparent has been received in every part of the world over which the Union Jack waves. The King's State and social duties have been performed with no less tact and dili- gence than distingUished his august mother, and in these he has been assisted to the uttermost by his gracious Consort, so that their loyal subjects throughout the Empire have every cause to say with ardour

THE K!NG'S CROWN.

The King's crown is a very pretty work of art, the materials whereof it is composed being costly and some- what hard to come by. Considered only as headgear it is not specially adapted to cope with the vagaries of the British climate, but bode its usefulness chiefly as a symbol of power and wide ebminion exceeding all pre- vious example. It is also a valuable asset, which in an emergency could be made to draw many ducats from Shyleck's treasury, though so far it has not been utilised in that way. There is a tremendous amount of huatlicg about the putting of it on the King's bead— mere than there would be about the taking of my bead from off my shoulders, which hardly seems fair. But as fairness was never characteristic of this world, and doesn't seem in the way to hemorne se, I shall rot afflict myself about a trifle such as that. Besides, my story is concerned with the parting from three crowns, not the gaining of One.

There have been numerous instances of asking com- pelled to part from his crown, and not infrequently his head has had to go with it; but at No. 158, St Michael's Rill, Bristol, lives Mr Robert Winter—not a king, it is true, but a respected tradesman—who recently parted from three crowns, and is infinitely better for the ex- change he then made. Mr Whiter thus describes the transaction : " Early last year I began to suffer from indigestion,and about two hours atter eating experienced intense pain. Constipation, its usual accompaniment. had for a long time troubled me. After even the lightest meal, my agony was sometimes so extreme that I was compelled to leave the shop and rsit or lie down until it passed away. Many a night I have got up out of bed and walked about the streets, vainly seeking to forget my trouble. I became peevish and disagreeable,and was so wretched that I didn't wish to speak to any one. Certainly, life was hardly worth living. I was attended by no less than three doctors, but derived no benefit from any- thing they gave me. After months of useless doctoring a neighbour said he was sure Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup would do me good, and having hall a bottle of it in the house he gave it to me. My neighbour was right. I spent fifteen shillings (three crowns) in all upon Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup, but it was well worth fifteen pounds to me, for it left me without a single symptom of indigestion." These words were written on January 3rd, 1902, and Mr Winter is as legitimately to be congratulated on the happy disposal of his three Crowns as is King Edward on rns accession. o one.

THE EXAMINER.

JUNE 26th. 1932.

loyal population averse to revolution *min whorl it presses keenly for reform.

nu truth is, indeed, that we have left so far behind us the bad old days when the Doxa of WKLLINGTON, who had saved his country from IciaroLEoic, saved her again THE GAIN AND from the Chartists, that we THG RISK, are in danger of forgetting

the foundations on which our content and our prosperity repose. There is no change in the popular temper, and no sign of a. change. But if the beginnings of reaction—which are all too obvious—were to go a little further, who can say what evils they might bring in their train? We have developed our free institutions so far, we accept them so much as a matter of course, that we have almost ceased to value them. But year after year, and with increasing rapidity since the late war began, Parliament has been losing its power, its vigilance, and its independ- ence. The Government absorbs the time of the House. The closure is used with appalling frequency.. The Estimates are scamped, and, while scores of millions are voted at a sitting, the theory of pub- lic control of public money is fast becoming a fic- tion. The Cabinet is able to exert a discipline over its supporters which is quite new in political life, and even within the Cabinet itself power is. more than ever concentrated in very few hands. We are less a democracy and more an oligarchy than ever we were before. It is the price we pay for our Imperialism and for our attitude to Ire- land. Nor in this connection can one forget that we have taken a step backward from the very policy of Free Trade which built up the content- ment and the prosperity of Queen VICTORIA'S reign. It is a sinister omen that the Parliament which adjourns to see King EDWARD crowned, breaks off its deliberations on the first step that is designed to restore the Corn ~Laws to their old place on the Statute-book.

THE contrast of the two coronations, is nowhere more apparent:than in our relations to ous Colonies. India at the last Coronation was still a vassal of a commercial Company,and ripe EMPIRE alca for the Mutiny which came later. To-day there is the shadow of famine; but at least it is not for private profit that we govern the great dependency. Canada was on the verge of the rebellion which came a few years after. To-day she is loyal because autonomous. Aus- tralia—a great free Commonwealth—was then a penal settlement. It is the grant of self-govern- ment which has made these young nations our enthusiastic allies, and the Coronation an Im- perial festival. But here, too, it is needful to sound a warning note. The Cape in 1837 was a troubled Crown Colony. TO-day unhappily, we are faced by a powerul agi- tation which seeks to destroy its coasti- tion and undo the great work of a great reign. Dr JAMESON is leading the movement, as though one raid, one hasty effort to do a great wrong to achieve a little right, were not enough for a single man. And, te complete the survey, there is Ireland—poorer weaker than she was, but no less disloyal because her population has dwindled by half. Not by going backwards;not by forgetting the secret of our success, can we hope to make the new reign brighter than the last. Could we but complete the great work of emancipation, and make Ireland and South Africa as free and as contented as autonomous Canada and New 79a-

CROWN-.

land, we might look forward fearlessly to the future—nay, we might hope even for the realisa- tion of the great dream which would convert an Empire that still rests in some of its provinces on force into a free federation,of loyal and self-govern- ing States.

The Coronation has its shadows after all. We can dwell on the peace, which seems so promising. We can rejoice in the loyalty of our great Colonies, which helped us so generously in the time of need. But the prospect is not all fair. There is the Cape, where the Constitution is in danger; Ireland, which celebrates the occasion in a gigantic national prosecution; and now it is India, where, after four years of practically continuous famine, a fifth seems imminent. There is little doubt that the calamity is at hand. The rains have failed,and the danger is officially admitted. The last famine ex- tinguished just one million lives, and no one can predict what this may accomplish. It is easy, of course, to blame the weather. Drought,of course, produces scarcity in every agricultural country. But it does not lead to such famines as these unless the peasantry is abjectly poor. Three-halfpence a day will keep an Indian alive in moderate comfort. But it is just that three-halfpence which he lacks. In the old days before the British occupation the rainfall was quite as erratic as it is now, but the peasant had his stores of grain, which tided hitn over a bad season. If that failed him there was the village money-lender, who would give him credit. But to-day the peasant lives from hand to mouth, and he exhausts his credit with the money-lender in order to meet the demands of the tax-gatherer. The burden of maintaining our army and our frontier policy has proved too much for him. One has:to remember that however severe these famines are they are always partial and local. There is always grain enough in India—indeed, even in famine years much is exported. The trouble is that the peasant is too poor to buy grain if his own yields fail to produce their crop. One cannot feel quite easy at the persistent refusal of the Indian Government to investigate the question. The Education Bill is inV,an extremely perilous position. In the first place,it is quite clear that it cannot possibly pass this session, and it is fairly certain that Parliament will be invited to meet again in November to consider it. Much may happen before 1%4.17mi-then Meantime several interesting revolts are in progress on the Conser- vative benches, and even if the bill should pass in some form or other it is a matter for spec ulation. what that form will be. -First of all, the staunch Tory wing, the country party, is resolved that it will not permit the imposition of a rata for any object so unnecessary as e.dac tion—even clerical education. To a deputation from this wing Mr Balfour has been .exceedingly politc, and though he promised nothing definite it is probable enough that some concession, will be made. The othe revolt is much more s. erious. No less than eighty Ministerial members,including some Liberal-Union ists, some Nonconformists, and not a few "Low Churchmen, have sent Mr Balfour a very decided ultimatum, which insists that the Bill mast be so amended as to secure real popular control. Need- less to say, this is exsctly what the Church party, with Lord Hugh Cecil at their head, are resolved to prevent. It is curious to watch how this revolt is met by the loyal members of the party. One is reminded of Mr. Chamberlain's old saying that Lord Salisbury never listened to the voice of the people unless it was hiccuped to him from the public-house. The " Standard " is quite undis- mayed. What are eighty Nonconformists, were or less, even when they happen to be -Coionis?! There are eighty Irish Catholics on the other sl of the House whose votes can be relied upon. " is doubtful whether the Irish members love the Bill more than they hate the Government. if in the end it really should be passed by thel'a votes it will be high time to agitate for Isiera Rule, not so much for Ireland as for England,

THE KING'S ILLNESS.

There has been no such sensational or tragic news in our recollection as that which startled and horrified a rejoicing, happy nation soon after noon yesterday. Like a veritable bolt from the blue came the sadden and totally unexpected intimation that His Majesty the King was seriously ill and that the Coronation—the event of a lifetime, to which millions of Britain's subjects had been looking forward with eagerness for weeks past —was in- danitely postponed. In credulity, tinged with alarm and suspense,was the feeling which filled the mind when the news was first -bruited abroad, Very soon, however, there was no room for doubt, and the sad intelligenca was confirmed. Official bulletins came pouring in rapidly, and it was found to be indeed a fact admitting of no question that he King was strickon down with an internal malady which rendered the crowning ceremony utterly impossible for some time to come. The national disapp ointment is keen in theiextreme,while the dislocation of arrangements and the pecuniary losses unavoidably sustained are well-nigh appalling to contemplate. But this aspect of the situatio n must soon— if it has not already—give way to one of deep sorrow at the blow which has fallen upon the Royal family. The sympathy of all must go out to their Majesties from full hearts. and the prayer of the nation will be that by the help of Providence a nd the skill of physicians the King may soon recover and be blessed with that robust health and strength we f °miry imagined was his until quite recently.

Locally, consternation reigned supreme for a time, but the opinion soon became general that to proceed with the cale brations arranged for would be far from seemly, as well as in a sense ridiculous. At a meating of the committee held last evening, a general abandonment of the celebrations organis ad for to-morrow was agreed to unanimou sly notwithstanding the fact that the sacrifice entailed is enormous. Such a decision will meet with the approval of the towns- people, we are confident,for their loyalty is not of a selfish type. The day will be allowed to pass quietly by, sery ing only to increase the regret and sorrow which predominate in every heart.

===== 0002601_19020627_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
TWO CORONATIONS.

THE state of mind in which the nation approached King EDWARD'S Coronation— now unhappily postponed —differs amazingly I fro aci that which prevailed when Queen VICTORIA cam's to th 0 throne. EDDWARD VII 1 inherits a deeply-root ed regard for the throne, which is equally diffused among all classes and parties. He profits by the private virtues and the political wisdom of his mother. It was quite otherwise in her case. There had been little in the private life or the public behaviour of King WILLIAM to modify the clisgust which the profligacy and the•brutality of GEORGE IV. had inspired in every thoughtful subject. Loyalty was a matter of controversy in the thirties, a real dividing line, To-day it is a matter of course. Then it was diffi- cult to be a Radical without a more or less open espousal of Republicanism. There were still Republicans among us, indeed when Mr. CHAMBERLAIN was a hot-headed young man. The working classes, when they were not utterly brutalised by the hardness of their lot were as revolutionary as any of the Socialist parties on the Continent to-day. The QUEEN her- elf was for a time profoundly unpopular, and it was the qualities which we most learnt to prize 1 which won her that cruel eminence. Her gentle- ness , her refinement, her love of the arts were all turned into ridicule. One may find in print. sellers' and curiosity shops the most brutal carica- tures of, this period, which made merry at the expense of her quiet family life and her Consort's devotion to music and painting. Her carriage used to drive amid a chilling silence through the London streets which fifty years later were packed with devoted crowds. The Tory party made no secret of its disloyalty,mainly because it distrusted her :innate Liberalism ; and there was even a moment when DAN O'CONNELL was her most chivalrous champion. All thathas changed, and the change personifies the progress of the reign. We are sufficiently educated to prefer a Sovereign more cultured than the rough WILLIAM, more res- ponsible than the reckless GEORGE. But, above all, it is the extension of the suffrage and the re- peal of the cruel Corn Laws which have given us

HOW WARRINGTON CELE- BRATED THE LAST CORONATION.

--- Interesting Retrospect.

There are few Warringtonians living to-day who can furnish the present generation with a clear and accurate account of the festivities which took place in the town on the occasion of the Corona- tion of the late Queen. That historic event to3k place on the 28th of June, 1838. Even the oldest amongst us were children at the time and the recollections of childhood are not always the most reliable. Warrington was at that time without its local newspapers and so far as we can learn there is not a single Warrington record in print of the celebrations in honour of the late Queen's Corona- tion. The only document in the Warrington Museum referring to the event is a small card giv- ing the programme of the day's celebrations. Two processions were organised, one commencing at 830 a.m of children only, sad another at 10.30 of townspeople, including the Trades and Friendly Societies. Toe programme, which is issued by "Hope, printer," reads as follows HER 11ADISTY'S CORONATION. DIURBDAY, JUNE 28vrr, 1838. The following will he the order of the processions Procession of the children at half-past 8 o'clock in the morning Town Crier, the apparitors of the church, the churchwardens, constables and overseers Band (Blue Coat Boys) 10 Wesleyan School 1 Blue Coat School 11 Friars Green Chapel 2 The Ladies' School Schools 3 St. Paul's School 12 St. John's School 4 Orford School 13 Stepne, School 5 St James School 14 Salem 3chool 6 Bank Quay School 11 Friars' Green School 7alWildersPool land Stockton 16 Primitive3dethodist Shoo/ e Lt h School 17 Roman Catholic School 8 Grappenhall School Band 18 Baptist Chapel Fchool and National School 19 Workhouse Children 9 Parish Church School

BYLASIDd, jun No chronicler has left us an accoent of these interesting proceedings but fortunately the late Mr Robert Davies penned a few of his recollections twelve months before his death, "I well recol- lect," Mr Davies wrote to a correspondent, "the Queen's accession and Coronation, but being at that time (the Coronation I mean) only eleven years old, I cannot speak of anything beyond the proces- sion. My prominent recollection is of the gentlemen of the town, my father included, riding on horse- back,the butchers doing the like in their blue coats the glassinakers walking in glass top hats, the printers with a printing press on a waggon printing bills as they went, our houses ornamented with laurel, gilt crowns, &c., bands and all the rest of it. But it must be remembered that there was no head then of the town, no police, no town hall, and that private effort stimulated log a voluntary committee was the only means of working the ceremony Mr Beamont was, I believe, the bon treasurer of the Coronation Committee. The county magistrates (there was then no borough commission of the peace) were, perhaps, the heads of the town. There were then no police, the four constables (always tradeemen:of the town appointed at the Court Leet) and their deputy Jones and his assistant Joynson being the sole guardians. No uniforms were known except on the town badman, who sported a blue coat with a scarlet collar, so there was nothing to make a show. There were no news- papers in the town, even my father contenting him- self with Bell's Weekly Messenger once a week. All newspapers were 6d. apiece, and I do not sup- pose that there was a Warrington record in print of Queen Victoria's corenation. I recollect the Coronation of William IV., and the public rejeicings on Cockhedge, which I saw perched ae a child on the shoulders of one of my father's clerks, and very I much frightened I was by the rough horse play which prevailed in that day of practical jokes : for instance,a lot of boys were eating scalding porridge fora wager staniing in a cart,a 'tumbril' cart, when someone pulled out ths ikekks' peg,causing the cart to fly upwards and capsizing all the boye,porringers and porridge in one struggling heap. Pigs were also turned out with soaped tails to be the prize of the first catcher. The successful man generally caught, I was told,the animal by getting hold of its tail in his teeth. Happily the days are gone by for such horseplay." From this interesting letter we are able to enter somewhat into the spirit of the time and the resder is able to form a pretty good idea of what trio festivities were like. There are a few points in connection with the proceedings which are worthy of notice. From the programme printed above it will be seen that the number of schools taking part in the procession including those in the dietrict was 19. At the forthcoming celebrations in connection with the King's Coronation 42 schools will participate. This number does not include several of the village schools which were included in the procession of 1838. A comparison of the number of schools affords striking evidence of the growth of the town during the late Queen's reign and is further proof of a general desire to instil Christian principles in the minds of the young. Tne Stepney school and S,lem school are now united under the title of Wycliffe school. In looking over the list of trades, the reader will be struck with the number and variety of trades represented in 'she procession of 64 years ago.Warrington has still kept up its reputation in this respect and the number has now been more than trebled. The only trades which the town appears to have lost entirely since 1838 are those of tallow chandling and sail-cloth making and both these trades have declined in consequence of newer methods in illumination and navigation. The illuminations which are tfa take place during this week's Coronation festivities will be on a ecale which was unknown at the last Corona- tion. In fact, there were probably no illumina- tions at all in Warrington in 1838 The public at any rate were notreguesteedotnotaiilniusmtihne modest ethdeesirt premises, for the programme recommendation to the inhabitants " to decorate the front of their houses With evergreens and flowers on the morning of the 28th inst." Some- thing more lavish is expected from the inhabitants of to-day. but it is questionable whether the decora- tion in many instances will be more effective than the evergreen and flower decoration of 1838. EAR LEST OWN & NEWTON

CADETS OF TEMPERANCE PICNIC.

The annual picnic in connection with the Cadets of Temperance, Samuel Morley branch, took place on Saturday afternoon, the party left Earlestown about 3 o'clock for Lea Green, to the number of about 100, in charge of Mr Pearson and Mr Judson, On arrival there a walk through the country brought them to Mickleh-al Green which they reached about 4 o'clock. A field had been kindly placed at their disposal by Mr Rawlins' and after tea they disported themselves in the field until train time. Various sports were indulged in, and the weather being fine the youngsters had a most enjoyable time. They left Lea Green for home about 9 o'clock much pleased with their outing. WARGRAVE CHURCH CHOIR PICNIC.

The annual picnic in connection with this choir took place on Saturday, the destination this year being Colwyn Bay. Tne choir,accompanied by the Rector, Rev J Carson, left Earlestown by the 8 46 train, and reached Colwyn Bs.y at 11. 9. The weather was of a beautiful description, an] as a preliminary to the day's enjoyment the party par• took of breakfast at Cartmell's Hotel. Coarabancs were afterwards requisitioned and a drive through the delightful country to Bodnant Hall was not the least portion of one of the pleasantest outings the choir bad had, the gardens at the Hall being a blaze of floral beauty. On returning to Colwyn Bay dinner was partaken of at Cartmelrs Hotel, and at 618 the party entrained for home. An hour and a half's halt at Chester enabled the party to view the city, and home was reached at-9 33. Mr T Martin, organist and choirmaster, and Mr R H Carson, as secretary had charge of the arrangements.

At the County Borough Police Court on Monlay, Michael Jeffers, 9, Neston-street, and James Haw- kins, 19, Lord Nelson-street, were charged with having committed a breach of the peace by fightigg in Factory-lane on the 16th inst. The Chief Con- stable caid a communication was made to him by a magistrate and he sent a police officer to Factory- lane. It was found that a somewhat sanguinary tignt had taken place there. The defendants had been in a public-house and from jesting the talk turned to fighting and they went into the street and bad a savage and disgraceful fight. punishing one another severely. A man named Isaac Smith incited them to fight and was as much to blame if not more than either of thsm.—i)efendants were bound over to keep the peace and ordered to pay the costs, 178 each.—A warrant was issued for the apprehension of Smith. 30,000 restored Pbyrlig ohR a vle6 rc xtheir s sight tr e Manchester. A Dishonest Errand Boy. Herbert Carter aged nine years, was sentenced to three strokes with the birch at the Police Court yesterday, fop stealing 3s, the money of William BeSWiCk, ironmonger 45z.c, last Saturday.—Prisoner was employed by the prosecutor on Saturday's only at a stall in the market. Last Saturday Mr Bes- Wick gave him 3s to take to his shop in Batter- market-street, together with a basket and a note. WARRINGTON CORONATION

ITEMS.

Treat to Workhouse Inmates.

At the Warrington Workhouse the inmates will have an enjoyable time on Coronation day. Breakfast will consist of coffee and bread and butter ; dinner of roast meat, potatoes, greens, fruit pies or plum podding, mineral waters, ale and fruit ; tea of sweet cake and tea—extras to consist of tobacco and snuff. The olscers will also be given a treat,and the female lunatics will have the rare luxury of a drive in the country. The Workhouse will be decorated. The suggeation that extra outdoor relief shoold be given in respect of the Coronation will not be adopted in the Warrington Union as the outdoor poor will be provided for in their respective parishel.

The Proposed Special Service. The TOWN CLERK has written to the Rector (Rev Canon Willis) as follows ,—" I am directed by the Mayor to inform you that he has conferred with other members of the Council with respect to your invitation to attend Divine Service on the Sunday after Coronation day. The Mayor wishes me to thank you for the invite- tiea and to state that owing to special services being arranged for in other places of worship on that day it will not be Convenient for many members of the Coun- nil to attend the Parish Church. Under these circure- Rances his Worship regrets that he /cannot accept the nvitatien.

Traffic in Coronation Week.

With reference to the traffic in Warrington on the two days of the public demonstrations the following orders have been made by the Town Council :—That on the ' occasion of the public proyescion of Friendly Societies and others on Thursday, the 26th June, 1902, no cart, carriage, tram car, lorry or any vehicle (except lorries or other vehicles used ire the procession, and mail carts), and no horse (except horses taking part in the proces- sion) shall be allowed to remain in or travel along any of the following streets between the hours of 9 am and 12 30 p m, viz. : Bridge-street, Buttermarket-streot, Church-street. Crofticl-street, Froghall-lane, Horsemar- ket-street, Irlam-strent, Mersey-street, Sankey-street, Tanners-Isne, Winwick-road, and Winwick-strert. Legb- street, Mersey-street, Parker-street, Sankey-street, Scotland-road, Wilson-Patten-street, and Winmarleigh- street. It is satisfactory to be able to state that the work of laying the tram rails in Church-street and relaying toe setts will be completed in time to prevent interference with the processions.
