MATTERS OF M OMENT A Provincial Letter.

Lord Kitchener, now on his way home, was fifty- two yesterday, being born on the 24th of June,lBso, and the circumstance is alluded to here as furnish- ing heads of families in the provinces whose eons may have during the past two years or more de- veloped a fancy for a military life, with a very notable example. Lord Kitchener is indubitably the an to whom we as Britons most owe the ter- mination of the war in South Africa. Both he and Lord Milner deserve congratulatione,and the nation read with pleasure the other day the gracious terms in which the King thanked both diplomatist and soldier. It may at once be conceded that since Lord Kitchener lended in South Africa in the early part of 1900, he has personally directed no great battle. Probably toe last great fight in whien he participated was that at Paardeberg,when Cronie was compelled to surrender ; but the " quick— silvering "of the Boers imposed on Lord Kitchener a far mere trying series of ta-ks. He was not only compelled to fight the Boers on their own terms and in their territory throughout a vast extent of country, but he had to arrange for the protection and provisioning of practically a whole nation. The , constant strain of active service for two years, in a semi-tropical climate, can scarcely have failed to impose physical exactions of the most severe char- acter, while the unrelieved mental strain must necessarily have been no less trying. Through all these, however, Lord Kitchener has passed almost scatheless. The risks of war have passed him by ; the deadly miasma of fever has left him untouched ; he has scarcely experienced an hour's illness ; and his stern and unremittiug devotion to the task before him has provided the British army, as well as the sons of Britons genet- ally,with a glorious illustration of what unflagging perseverance and attention to duty may acCesplieh Particularly candid critics even, on the continent, have been constrained to admire Lord Kitchener's resourcefulness, his pertinacity, and his personal bravery. It is well-known that Lord Kitchener is no popularity-hunter, but is one who finds his highest reward in the consciousness that he has performed his duty to the best of his ability. Yet, as the most notable military figure that the twen- tieth century has produced, he will have to submit to the penalty of hero-worship as soon as he again sets foot on his native shore.

The chief subject for regret at the outset has been that these thousands of foreigners could not be very favourably impressed by the climatic con- ditions under which the Londoner lives, moves and has his being. The longest day of the year came land went, and the first day of summer according to the calender, did the same, and yet a lingering 1 winter somewhat chilled the lap of June. The Iweather-wise, of course, attached importance to the fact that the fulness of tbe moon on Saturday night synchronised with a climatic serenity that had in 1902 been much more the exception than the rule. From this conjunction of circumstances the optimist wove the most favourable visions ot an ideal Coronation week. On the other band, there circulated about the same time ceriain rumours as to the King's health, of:a kind that ought to be un- sparingly rebuked and condemned. As it happened, they were not allowed to remain officially uncon- tradicted long. They were declared, by the highest authority, to be absolutely devoid ef foundation. It is pleaded that they may have arisen from the fact that the Royal ball at Windsor was postponed and that the visit to Eton had been cancelled. These postponements, however, had reference to the death of the King of Saxony. King's Edward's health, it was stated was quite satisfactory. So, also, was that of tbe Premier,the Marquis of Salis- bury. The Prime Minister had been somewhat ailing during last week, and, acting upon medical advice, had remained indoors. But the illness was not such as to interfere with his State duties, and happily proved to be of but a temporary charac- ter.

DISGUITING ALLEGATiOTT2I;:IitEMPLARY PUNISHMENT.— James Bellwood was summoned at the instance of Elizabeth Banner, married woman, for having used abusive language towards her on the IGth June, whereby a breach of the peace was occasioned. Mr Knowles prosecuted on behalf o the complainant —The evidence showed that both parties lived in Pettiest-lane and that up to some time ago they were on intimate terms. The defendant at the end of January in the absence of her husband, a sailor, attempted to assault Mrs Banner. She resisted and screamed fcr assistance the defendant after- wards apologising for his conduct. The complainant told her husband's brother of the attempted outrage and he ad- vised her to saylnothing about it to her husband who was of a hasty disposition.—At a later data the defendant then used someldisgusting language toward her in the hearing of a public—A number of witnesses ware called on both sides, the Bench inflicting a fine of 30s and costs, or in default a month's inaprisonment.—Arising out of the same ease, Mrs Banner was summoned by the wife of the last defendant, Bessie Bellwood, for using threats to hen—Evidence was produced in support of the chance, but the Bench dismissed the case, the complainant having to pay the costs. SAVING UP Fult THE CORONATION.—A. boy named Albert Turner, Was charged with stealing £2 Is lid, and a postAl order be, belonging to Reuben Barman manager to tho Manchester Pnrnishing Company..--The evidence showed that the prosecutor had been callecting money which he put in the till. A FARMER SHOT NEAR WIGAN.

Th. death is reported of Mr John Dean, of Manor House Farm, Crawford village, near Wigan. His wife heard the report of firearms, and found silr Dean sitting in a chair with wounds on the head. It is supposed he was taking down the gun whoa it fell and was dis- charged. The deceased gentleman's daughter met her death by beieg accidentally shot a few years ago.On Monday Mr S Brighouse, county coroner,held an inquest on the body. Mr J Dean, solicitor, of Liverpool,brother at the deceased, was present on behalf of the relatives. Mn Dean stated that on hearing the report of firearms she went down into the kitchen and found that her hue band had reached down a gun, which had been discharged, the shot entering his neck. Her husband was sitting in a chair, and the gun was near him.—Dr. Brinskill, of Rainford, deposed that the wound was a large one, and the shot had fracture 4 the skull. Tbe muzzle most have been about Ein, from the deceased when the gun was discharged. He was of opinion that Mr Dean could not possibly have shot him- self.—A verdict of " Accidental death " was returned, and the jury passed a vote of sympathy with the family,

Captain Freeman, of the se. Roddam, was on Monday in Lloyd's Committee room, London, presented with the Lloyd's silver medal for meritorious services in the way he got out of St Pierre with his ship on the occasion of the great volcanic explosion. The chair. man of Lloyds, Mr J H Lusaombe, told the recipient that be felt it an honour to have the privilege of presenting him with this token of their appreciation of hie services, and said he was a striking example of the race whom they were all proud of,the British sailor. Captain Freeman briefly returned thanks, and was then taken to the underwriting room by the chairman, where he was received with great cheering by " thelmemberS , and subscribers.

THE MAYOR'S CORONATION BANQUET.

'Twas only fitting that among the multitudinous methods of celebrating the Coronation of King Edward the Seventh, the Mayor of Warrington should entertain to dinner the Town Council, Borough officials, and magistrates. This he did on Monday evening when Caere was a select gathering in the Council Chamber of the Town Hall to partake of His Worship's hospitality. The chamber itself is admirably suited for such a func- tion, but it possesses a serious drawback in the matter of size. The diners had ample accommoda- tion, but the space round the tables is barely sail,- cient for the busy waiters to move about in, while it [is to be questioned if the cubic air space is suffi- cient for a full attendance of guests. Certainly when all the available air is impregnated with cigar smoke there does not seem any too much for the lungs, but this is an inconvenience easily borne under the circumstances. Some day, no doubt, the Town Hall will possess a chamber admirably adapted for its multifarious purposes. On Mon- day: evening, however, the Council Chamber looked very attractive with its handsomely arranged tables, simple, choice decorations, and lace- curtained windows, through which the bright sun of a typical June afternoon streamed gloriously. Flowers were very largeb in evidence,the Mayor's gardens supplying a number of choice white lilies and orchids. At one end of the Chamber was the only suggestion of the Coronation—apart from the menu cards—and this took the form of a number of gay flags and ensigns surrounding a regal-look- ing crown in the centre of the west. wall. There was nothing obtrusively ornate,but the scene was bright and cheerful and befitted the occasion. The Mayor, of course, occupied the central posi- tion, and had on his right and 1 aft respectively, Alderman Sutton (senior Alderman) and the Rev Ccinon Willis (the Mayor's Chaplain). There were also present :—The Deputy Mayor (Alderman Roberts), Aldermen Bolton, Fairclough, Francomb, Shaw Green, Heaton, Hutchinson, and F W Monks;. Councillors Baird, Barton, Burton, Duncan, Ellison, James Evans, John Evans, Forshaw, Hulse, A J Jolley, A Jolley, Malkin, Pelcock, Pierpoiut, John Roberts, William Roberts, Rogers, Sampson. Shill- cock, Smethurst. Tinnion, and Wilkinson; Messrs William Owen, J.P., J C Rilgway, J.P., T Birchall, J.P., R T Fairclough, J.P., Dr Fox, J.P., J W Hay- ward, J.P., J Hepherd, J.P., Henry Thornton, J.?" S Wright, J.P., J It Young, J.P, Eager C Parr, R G Henshall (borough banker), H Isher wood (Editor " Observer "), B V Walker (Editor "Examiner "), J Lawton ("Guardian "),1 J Lyon Whittle (town clerk), J Fairhurst (borough treasurer), T Longdia (borough surveyor), J Deas (water engineer), L Talbot(chief constable),W S Haddoek(gas engineer), C Madeley (librarian), W H Grimsdale (electrical engineer), B Wilson (dentity town clerk), F F Whits (magistrates' clerk), and Nelson Jackson (humorist). The dinner was an excellent one in every respect, abounding in varied and seasonable dishes and fruits. Mr POwis is to be complimented on his catering for this eaceprionat event. ALARMING MEDICAL _REPORT ON

DEAFNESS;

At a late meeting of the Otoiogical Society an eminent mist made the startling report that out of 1,000 school childreratie found 520 afflicted by defective hearing. Another medical man also stated that he had detected a similar pry irtion of deaf children out of 200 exam- ined in a Livern3ol school. With such an alarming pro- portion of dear people growing up its our midst it is most important to call public attention to the necessit y for detecting the beginning of defective hearing. The medical profession in general are doing all they can in this direction but it has remained for the Drouet In,titute to sweep away the scientific verbiage with which aurists have surrounded the investigation of ear diseases and to give publcity to a description, in plain words, of simple tests of the hearing that enable any- one to detect the slightest degree of Deafness. This description, wi'zn much valuable information on the prevention of ear diseasea, the Care Of the ears, and opinions on the curability of deafness, have been em- bodied by the Drouet Institute in " The Journal for the Deaf," which is issued quarterly. This publication, which is devoted entirely to the interests of those who are aflicted by deafness, or by any diseases of the ear, nose, or throat, can be obtained free by post by addressing the Secretary, Drouet Insti- tute, Regent's park-road, Primrose Hill, London, N. W. The "Journal " also describes a Method of Treating deafness by external application that has been repeated- ly successful in curing ear diseases, and ia restoring hearing in cages pronounced incurable by the usual aural treatment. Those who are interested in knowing how to apply, this method will find that each " Journal " is accompanied by a Patient's Report Form,which enables anyone to receive full particulars as to the use of the treatment at home. Any advice lei correspondence is absolutely free. Those who desire to have the ear, nose and throat examined by a specialist are reminded that they can take advantage of the reduced railway fare at Coronation • time and visit the Drouet Institute. The fee for examination will remain, as usual, 59 during the regular consultation hours, 2to 4 p in, wee k- days, but those who may wish to be eiamined at some other hour of the day can arrange for a special consulta- tion by writing to the Secretary, Drouet Institute, Regent's Park-road, Primrose Hill, London. The Institute will be closed June 25th and 27th.

THE SEVEN EDWARDS.

EDWARD I.

The first King of England named Edward was in reality he who was surnamed "The Confessor," and to whom succeeded Harold, defeated and killed at Hastings by 'William of Normandy. But Edward the Confessor has never been known as Edward I. That title is given to Edward of Westminster (called Longshanks, because of the excessive length of his legs), who succeeded his father, Henry 111., in 1272, when he was thirty-three years of age. He reigned over England for thirty-five years, dying in 1307, and was the first of the later Angevin Kings more familiarly known as the Plantagenets. Edward I. was a wise and a good King, and his reign was characterised by great progress and success in reform of the laws of the land —in the first application of the Parliamentary principle to the government of the country, and in the extension of law and order throughout the kingdom. Edward I. was a man of great personal courage and resource. He had won his spurs on many a battlefield before his turn came to ascend the throne, and he had gained much experience and

wisdom from his travels. His coronation took place at Westminster, where he was crowned with his Queen. Eleanor of Castile, in 1274, and was unprecedented both in the splendour of its ceremonial and the popular rejoicings with which it was celebrated. Edward's first task in coming to the throne was the subjugation of Wales; but this Mr. 1 R. Green considered only the starting-point of the great work he had set himself of estab- lishing the judicature, the legislature, the Parliament—in fact, the scheme of national administration in which begins the constitu- tional England of to-day. The Statutes of Westminster and Gloucester, which he passed, dealt with land tenure • and that of Winchester provided for the defence of the country and the proper policing of towns and parishes. To conciliate the Welsh; whom he had conquered, he made ,his son prince of Wales—a title which has been borne by the heir to the English throne ever since.

===== 0002601_19020627_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
EDWARD 11.

Edward II succeeded his father, the first Edward, in 1307, and reigned for twenty years. He lacked all the great character- istics of his father, and his reign was the very antithesis of that which had preceded it. Edward 11. was the fourth son of the "Great Plantagenet," his three elder brothers having died while their father was still alive. He married the most beautiful woman in France, Isabella, daughter of Philip N. of France. But he systemati- cally neglected his Queen and his country for one Piers de Gaveston, a handsome Frenchman to whom he was passionately attached. Gaveston was beheaded later by the Earl of Warwick. Edward 11. is chiefly remembered for the defeat which he sustained at the hands of Robert Bruce at Bannockburn. An English army of 100,000 men, under the King, went north to subdue Scotland, but they came back considerably quicker than they went—at least, such of them as were not captured or slain. The battle of Bannock-

burn completely broke Edward's power in the kingdom; and the Barons, who had been opposing the King and his favourites, now controlled the country. The Earl of Lancaster was the most powerful man in the kingdom, and was a sort of medieval Pooh Bah—Lord High Everything. The King, however, found new favourites, the result of which was that his Queen came into league with the ;Mks, and Edward was driven from the kingdom and formally deposed, the chief charges against him being that he had followed evil counsellors, that he had neglected the business of the State, that he had lost Scotland, Gascony, and Ireland, and that he had slain and exiled good men. The unhappy monarch's end was like his life, miserable and tragic—he was murdered at Berkeley Castle on September 21st, 1327, by order of the Queen and her favourite, Mortimer.

===== 0002601_19020627_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
EDWARD 111.

Edward 111. (eldest son of the murdered and deposed King) came to the throne in troublous times at the age of fourteen. The earlier part of his reign was spent under the regency of the nobles, but the power remained in the hands of Queen Isabella and Mortimer. The latter was tyrannical and insolent, and when he had reached the age of nineteen Edward of Windsor, as he was called, decided on a bold stroke. Be had Mortimer arrested and beheaded, threw his mother into prison at Castle Rising, in Norfolk, and assumed control of the nation's affa!irs himself. Edward of Windsor reigned wisely and well for fifty years, and Hallam assigns to him the

title of "Father of English Commerce, because in his reign commerce became, next to liberty, the leading object of Parliament. But Edward was great also in war. He began what is known as the Hundred Years' War with France, and his name, and that of his son, the Black Prince, one of the most romantic figures in history, will ever be associated with the glorious victories of Cressy and Poictiers and the capture of Calais. His reign was remarkable for other things besides commerce and war, however, for it saw a great increase in the strength of Parliament, and especially of the Commons, and also the rise of a great ecclesiastical revolu- tion against the power of the Pope, which only ended in the Reformation. Before his death, which took place at Richmond in 1377, one year after his son, the Black Prince, had died, England lost all her French dominions except Calais, Bordeaux, and Bayonne.

===== 0002601_19020627_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
EDWARD IV.

We have to pass over a hundred years to 1461 before we meet with another Edward on the throne of England. In that year succeeded Edward IV., first King of the House of York, who was called to the throne by Parliament on the defeat of the Lancastrians. Like the third Edward, he was at home in war, and a week after he had been crowned he met and defeated the Lancastrians at Towton in York- shire in the battle of "Palm Sunday Field." On either side were about 60,000 men, and when the day was won, after six hours' fight- ing 33,000 men lay dead upon the field. Edward of York was immensely tall, standing a head above the nobles of his Court, and of great personal beauty. He was popular with his people, too, for he was genial and affable to them, and combined a splendid and lavish household with an interest and share in the commercial enterprise of the day. With all his geniality, however, he was a man of great passion, pitiless in anger, treacherous and heartless towards those he disliked, and of

Inflexible will when his ire was raised. His rule was the nearest approach to absolute tyranny, and during his reign the functions of Parliament were almost entirely suspended. He is said to have put to death no less than 1,400 persons—chiefly of high rank. His character was one of strange complexity, but his reign must be said in some respects to have strengthened immeasurably the com- mercial classes of England. The power of the Barons was broken by the Wars of the Roses, and the Guilds of Ironmongers, Merchant Taylors, Carpenters, Clothworkers, and others obtained from him their charters. It was in his reign, too, that William Caxton intro- duced the art of printing into England, and the King patronised him considerably. Edward IV.*4 reign was broken by the restora- tion in 1470 of Henry VI., but only for a short time, the battle of Tewkesbury restoring tan in tura.

EDWARD V,

Nothing can be more pitiful than the short reign of the child Edward V. of Westminster, who succeeded his father on the latter's death in April! 1483, at the age of twelve, was deposed in June the same year, having reigned only eleven weeks and one day, was never crowned, and died—probably murdered—in the Tower before he had'reached his thirteenth birthday,which would have been in November, 1488. At the time of his succession the boy was being educated at Ludlow Castle. On the road to London (at Stony Stratford, in Watling-street) he was met by Richard, Duke

of Gloucester, afterwards Richard HI, alle the Duke of Buckingham, by whom he W 53. lodged in the Tower. He became the cent, of plots and conspiracies. Gloucester managet to get the boy's brother from the custody oi the Queen-Mother, who had sought sanctuart at Westminster, and induced the Lords ittia Commons to petition him to accept tbe Crown. The story of the murder of the two "Little Princes in the Tower "is familiar to every schoolboy, and, although there is fl! authentic record of it, the manner of their death and the authorship thereof are generalli accepted.

EDWARD VI.

The next Edward who reigned in England —and the last until the present Sovereign came to the throne—lived in troublous times., like so many of his predecessors. But 1131 troubles were of a different character. The conspiracies and plots of the barons, the cloak of knightly strife, had given place to the intrigues of the ecclesiastics and the strife et, the churches. Edward VI. was the son ei Henry VIII. by Jane Seymour, and his father died when he was but nine years of ags • Henry had left careful provisions for • Regency by Council, but the power vae usurped by the Duke of Somerset, whe assumed the title of Protector. Religious aa social upheavals followed one another in quiet

succession, and the revised edition of Oil Prayer Book was a constant source of troub ,I•• But Edward was a youth of parts, lovi", ' learning, and gentle in disposition, if perh&P's , a little weak, as his submission to the will °I Warwick when the latter demanded Or, sacrifice of his uncle, Somerset, demonstrat Edward VI. is chiefly remembered for tork noblest measure of his time—the endowniell' of eighteen grammar schools, which still fall an important part of the higher educatiol system of England. Had he lived Englo,, might have been saved many of the horro"' which she suffered in the Marian persecutioot, But his health waned, and in 1553, still_," youth of but fifteen years, he died, leavoill' behind him chaos. _

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The death of the Kiog of Saxony has placed the Court in mourning, necessitating the abandonment of the great ball at Windsor Castle, but it has not other- wise been permitted to interfere with the Coronation festivities. With these over, the London season will quickly wane and people are already making their plans for the summer and autumn. Goodwood and Cowes are nOW all that remain of the principal attractions for People of fashion and in the ordinary course the King. and other members of the Repel Family will be present at both events. The King proposes to take advantage of the yachting festival for going on a short cruise,before taking his usual holiday on the Continent,but the Queen Will probably accompany Prince and Princes Charles on their return to Denmark. Princess Charles of Denmark has become quite skilled 'tithe management of her motor car, which was a pre- sent to her from the King The Princess is, like her sister the Duchess of Fife, fond of open air life, and is in fact never at a loss for occupation either in or out of doors. Few members of the Royal Family have so many hobbies, and Princess Charles amuses herself with Photography, fancy work, and chess, whilst she fa an excellent linguist speaking and writing even that diffi- cult language, Russian, so that good-reading is always at her command. It is however, the outdoor life that Princess Charles so much appreciates in this country. She regards Appleton Hall, near Sandringham, as her toiletry house, and here she may be seen riding,cycling, Playing croquet, and also driving in her new motor car. The four Duchesses whom the Queen appointed to attend her Majesty at the Coronation, were not selected from any fixed rule of precedence, but from personal choice. This great honour was accorded to the Duchess of Marlborough, the Duchess of Montrose, the Duchess of Portland, and the Ductless of Sutherland. As is well- known the Duchess of Marlborough wee Miss Consuelo Vanderbilt, and, amongst the many American women Who have become peeresses in this country, she has become most suecesstul as a charming hostess. The Duclasse of Montrcse was one of the beautiful Miss Grahams of Netherby, and but lately celebrated her silver wedding. She is almost as well-known, both in Londow and Scotland, as is the Duchess of Sutherland, for her philanthropic work, and her unceasing en- deavours to promote the welfare of the less fortunate classes. The Duchess of Portland who was MisslWinifred Dallas-Yorke, is scarcely so well-known for her social Work among the poor, but she always takes charge of the Duke's winnings on the turf, which she devotes to charities on the Welbeck estate. Much has been written about the premier peeresses at the Coronation, and it ,is singular that, whilst the Buckets, of Somerset. who appeared as the premier uchess of England, is a Scotswoman, the premier Marchioness—The Marchioness of Winchester—is an Irishwoman. The Duchess of Somerset was a Miss Mackinnon, a member of the old Scottish family of Mackinnon, of Skye. She is a tall handsome woman, anti succeeded with her husband to the strawberry leaves, about 8 years ago. She has no children, but is deleted to philanthropic work,and is not only an accom- Pliehed writer and musician, but also sketches and *elute, and sometimes exhibits beriwork. The Mar- ehioness of Winchester is also one of the most interesting Of Society women. She was the widow of the late Mr Garnett when, still quite young, she became the wife of the Marquis of Winchester, ten yeses ago. It is thirty-seven years since Mrs Kendal made her Ant appearance as a girl of 16, on the London stage, ziod it must therefore be admitted that she knows some- thing of the subject when she writes to a London paper on the desire to join the theatrical profession which tleminetes so large a number of people. Mrs Kendal , hos had a good deal of experience of these aspirants, ` nod seems to have been struck chiefly by the " amazing Self "Confidence "of a larger proportion of them. Of „course she has discovered, as the majority of us do, that the most self-confident people, are, generally, most in- nompetent, and she refers in her own delightful way to the ludicrous failure of some of those who were excep- • 9onally conteeted with themselves. At the same time it does not invariably follow that the amateur who fails *hen called upon to recite in coldeblood before a ti die- o • ' Maisled actor or actress, is totally devoid of talent. +bore was tor example, an exeremely clever amateur • actor who obtained an opportunity of reciting before Barry Sullivan. Ine was nervous, and the result was Such a failure that Sullivan glancing scornfully at the , Man before him, exclaimed tsosomebody who was stand- ing near Take it away !" But Barry Sullivan was mistaken as was proved by subsequent events. In the columns ot Mr. Clement Scott's paper, there has been a discussion on the subject " Why Women won't Marry." The answer to this question is a simple one, viz, That women do marry. The Registrar-General in his last report points out that there has been a real • increase in the marriage-rate,which, during the past ten years has reached a higher figure than at any time since , the early seventies of the nineteenth century. The reason Why more women do not marry is because they cannot, there being so many more women than men in the popu- • 'Mien of this country. It would be more practical and Useful to initiate a discussion as to whether something Could not be done to afford women the opportunity of inarrying if they wished to do so. In many of our Possessions abroad, the male population is grestly in excess of the female, and the immigration of women Would be welcomed, If some kindly people in whom Women would feel confidence would take up the matter, ' the demand in one country might be met by the over- Supply in another, but it would be necessary to make it clear that the movers were considering only the welfare ' f our sex. Never have sunshades appeared more varied and ex- quisite than this year, and never surely have they been ln less demand. Shopkeepers are in despair concerning these and numberless other airy fairy accessories of the toilette, through which they will lose considerably,as the majority will have to find a place in the summer sales. Those blouses too, intended for day wear, and built with transparent yoke and sleeves, must prove another great lose, to the average draper. Had the weather been more reasonable, these would doubtless have gone off well among the lesser lights, who last year appeared to ,appreciate them so well, but so far few have risked their 'lves in this particular way. Occasionally when taking one's walks abroad—often in a keen north-east wind— one has met a woman apparently under the impression that the time was night, and the seen° of action a cosy Inwieg room, but such individuals (whose proper place es a lunatic asylum) have been few and far between. Sequins are enjoying as wide a vogue as evernhose in teuenished silver being the favourite of the moment. The burnished silver sequin it may be mentioned is consider- ,ably duller than the old silver specimen and much more uesirable, the pearly look it acquires in the process giv- ing it a deligbtfully refined appearance. In embroideries, Eteel is taking the place of silver, another move in the eight direction aEI. the latter is very apt to make the nicest and mot earefully thought out frocks look just a trifle vulgar.

THE FREE CHURCHES.

===== 0002601_19020627_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
FREE CHURCHMEN AND THE CORONATION.

For the first time in the history of England official representatives of the Free Churches were "commanded" to attend the Coronation of the Sovereign at Westminster Abbey. We believe that the editors of the leading Noncon- formist papers also received invitations to the ceremony. Unfortunately, the health of Principal Caleb Scott, the Chairman of the Congregational Union, is unsatisfac- tory, and Dr. Barrett was appointed ipe attend in his stead. Dr. W. T. Davison, the President of the Wesleyan Confereneik, is attending the Irish Methodist Conference, and Di. Thomas Allen was chosen as the repre- sentative of Ihe great Methodist body. Per- haps one is not very far wrong in suspecting that Dr. Davison, who is a shy, shrinking man, is not sorry that he has escaped the fatigue and nervous strain. King Edward VII. has no more loyal and devoted subjects than the Nonconformists of England.

===== 0002601_19020627_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
THE HEALTH OF DR. PARKER.

The health of Dr. Parker is again causing his friends the gravest anxiety. He still suffers severely from neuralgic pains and from his heart trouble, and his return to the pulpit of the City Temple is indefinitely postponed. The other. great Nonconformist preacher, Dr. McLaren, of Manchester, has also been in poor health, but we are glad to learn t hat his is merely a passing indisposition.

===== 0002601_19020627_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
A lINIOICISIE REVOLT?

The agitation against Site Education Bill is being maintained vigorously in all parts of the country, and it is now quttie ‘certain that, if the measure becomes law, many Nonconformists will go to prison or allow their goods to be seized rather than pay the rate. Meanwhile, there are very manifest signs of uneasiness among the supporters of the Government. Over eighty Unionist members of the House of Commons have signed a memorial to Mr. Balfoni requesting that , the public representation in the management of Voluntary schools (the Bill provides for one third) should be increased. If these memorialists have a good supply of iron in their blood, we should witness some very important developments. Mr. Bal- four's manner to the influential deputation from the National Free Church Council was courtesy itself, but he did not give them the faintest ground of hope.

MR. GEORGE TOULMIN, M.P.

The July Free Church Chronicle , contains an interesting sketch (with portrait) of Mr. George Toulmin, the new member for Bury. Mr. Toulmin, who is one of the proprietors of the Preston Guar- dian and Lancashire Daily Post, is superin- tendent of the St. Mary-street Wesleyan Sunday Spennl, Preston, and President of the Preston District rreu Federation. On the Sun- day after the election the scholars sent their superintendent a letter of congratulation. Mr. Toulmin's father was a member of the Society of Friends, and his father was a teetotaller even before the day when Livesey, of Preston, drew' up the first total abstinence pledge. Mr. Toulmin's maiden speech in the House was the subject of many compliments. The new Mem- ber is forty-five years, but looks ten years younger, and gives promise of Parliamentary distinction.

===== 0002601_19020627_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
TWO NOTABLE DEATHS.

It is interesting to note that the Rev. John Spurgeon, the father of the famous preacher, who has just died at a great age, commenced his pastorate only a few years before his son. Mr. John Spurgeon was for many years in busi-, ness before he definitely began his ministerial career in 1849. Charles Spurgeon began to preach in 1850 (when he was sixteen), was settled presently at Waterbeach, and in 1853 began his ever-memorable work in London. John Spurgeon for years exercised a useful, fruitful, and unobtrusive ministry at Tollesbury, Cranbrook, Fetter - lane (Fleet - street), and Islington. He has come to his grave in a full age, "like as a shock of corn cometh in in his season."

THE SUCCESSOR OP DR. GUINNESS ROGERS.

Rev. E. W. Lewis, M.A., has been recognised as successor of Dr. Guinness Rogers at Grafton- square Congregational Church, Clapham. At the gathering Mr. Lewis recalled his first meet- ing with Dr. Rogers. Many years ago Mr. Lewis and his brother were, as youngsters bound for Caterham School, placed by their father, a Derbyshire pastor, in charge of Dr, Guinness Rogers (who happened to be travelling south that day), for safe convoy to St. Pancras. The arrangement proved mutually unsatisfac- tory. The schoolboys were "overawed "by the great man's presence, and he, finding them in- sufficiently intelligent to be absorbed in the objects of interest he pointed outto them, buried himself in the Times, while they discussed means of escape. At Derby, under the pretence of buying a bath bun, the boys got cut of Dr. Rogers's sight, and, slipping into another car- riage, made the rest of the journey to St. Pancras, to their own relief, and (added Mr. Lewis) "to the even greater relief, I am sure, of Dr. Rogers." In Mr. Lewis a new force has been added to London Congregationalism.

Mr. Greenhough has been persuaded to re- consider his decision to resign the pastorate of Victoria-road Baptist Church, Leicester. His people are hopeful of retaining him.

WANTED-A REAL SPIRITUAL SERMON.

Rev. Thomas Hamer, the Chaplain at the Menston Asylum, appointed by the Bradford District Federation, narrates in a very touch- ing and Interesting account of his work that before it became his duty to attend every ser- vice he found on the reading-desk one Sunday morning the following note:

"SlB,—Attendants and nurses, and many of the patients who attend the service, °Yillustrations, as they are no twlongerhoeuyldn can't eant a real spiritual sermon where children. sermon y, S ot to-day, give us a good, comfortingand oblige "MANY."

THE QUEEN'S TEA TO DOMESTIC; SERVANTS. Great dissatisfaction is being expressed in Nonconformist circles at the manner of dis- tributing tickets for this function. So impor- tant an organisation as the Free Church Girls' Guild has been entirely ignored. The distribu- tion was superintended by the Bishop of London.

The Barons of the Cinque Ports are ta have charge of the four banners representing England, Ireland, Scotland, and the Union to be carried in procession In the Abbey. A bench is to be provided for the Barons, who take turns in holding the banners.

Ladies other than peeresses presf nt at the ceremony in the Abbey on Thursday must, it is stated, all be dressed in white. By this means an injudicious mixture of colour will be avoided, and the brilliancy of the peeresses' robes enhanced by the neutsality of the other ladies' attire.

The Royal Irish Constabulary will bo represented in the Coronation procession by a detachment of troopers, with Colonel Chamberlain its chief coin- mand,and the Dublin Metropolitan Pollee by twelve constables and a sergeant, in command of the Chief Commissioner, Colonel Ross of Bladensburg. The War Office has issued an order stating that the Bing directs that all officers on the active list witnessing the Royal processions on the 26th and 27th inst., shall appear in full dress. All other officers not covered by the terms of this order who are in possession of full-dress uniform are desired to wear it on these occasions.

71' 4.• announced in army orders that remission of ,ntences will be granted on Coronation Day to men convicted of comparatively trivial offewes, and that a general holiday will be given.

The magnificent State landau in which their Majesties will ride on Friday is essentially a British product alike in design, material, and workmanship. The panels of its immense body are purple lake, the underworks are painted vermilion picked out with gold, and the carved mouldings are gilt throughout. The best way to give in idea of its size is to state in detail its dimensions. Length 18ft. 61n., width 7ft. 2in., height from ground to roof Bft. 6in., diameter of front wheels 3ft. Bin., of hind wheels 4ft. 9in., with length of body on waist line 7ft. 6in. This is 12in. longer than the largest landau in the Royal establishment. It was decided to build a second carriage in order to give their Majesties' loyal subjects a better view of the King and Queen than would, of course, have been possible in the closed coach.

Of the twenty-eight Cavalry Regiments of the Line it will only be possible for one of these—the 21st Lancers—to take part in its entirety in the Coronation ceremonies. This regiment returned home from Egypt in 1899, and has up to the present been quartered in Ireland.

Great disappointment exists amongst the Irish military corps at present in camp at Aldershot in consequence of the decision of the Wat Office authorities to utilise only one hundred and fifty A.stin from each regiment for duty in London on the lioronation and Royal Progress days. Before leaving Ireland it was generally understood that every man would be required to proceed to the metropolis for the event of the year; and it is submitted by those in touch with the regiments that, inasmuch as 99 per cent. of the rank and file were never in London, it is a short-sighted policy to neglect this oppor- tunity of conveying to them a lasting impression concerning the greatness of the Empire, and the extent and variety of the Imperial ramifications.

There is not great gratification amongst the Free Churches at the invitations to Westminster Abbey and St. Paul's. Congregationalists and Baptists are to have only one representative each at Westminster Abbey. The Wesleyans are favoured, for both the President and the secretary of Conferenco have been invited. This meagre representation contrasts very badly with the large number of seats allotted to Nonconformists in the Abbey at the Diamond Jubilee. Then Congregationalists alone had forty seats. The arrangements for the thanksgiving service at St. Paul's on July 3rd do not seem to promise much better. At present the Dean and Chapter have provided only twenty seats for the whole of the Free Churches.

It is to be hoped that on the Coronation days large hats will be banished. They are really unsuitable to such a function, and look very unbecoming when disarranged, as they are certain to be on such an occasion.

A well-known railway official, perhaps the best authority on Continental traffic, says that the rail- ways with a Continental service are expecting a large increase of British tourists immediately the Coronation is over. After all the banqueting, feasting, revelling, and excitement resulting from the great event about to be celebrated, the baths and waters of Europe will be sought out in every direction as an antidote for all the bodily troubles with which modern luxury has endowed us.

'l'm London—bus companies, following on the suggestion of the cabdrivers, have decided to increase all fares. "There will," said Mr. Kingham, the secretary of the London General Omnibus Com- pany, "be t a penny fares during Coronation week. We have not yet decided on what the other increases shall be exactly, but they will most likely be double the present fares in all cases."

There will be twenty-eight cruisers and battle- ships at Gibraltar for the Coronation celebrations, drawn from Cyprus, Alexandria, Smyrna, and Malta.

The dais in the "theatre" of Westminster Abbey on which the thrones will be placed is to be covered with a worked Oriental carpet of great value. It was made by hand in the middle of the sixteenth century, but it shews no signs of fading and is extremely handsome. Another carpet which will attract much attention is the immense one specially made in the Midlands. It will be put in the nave, and will cover the whole of the floor. The colour is a rich dark blue, and the design includes the star of the Garter, the rose, thistle, and shamrock, and the Egyptian lotus.

The inmates of several large union workhouses are asking to be allowed to post their letters free on Coronation day, and—to stay in bed till nine a.m. 1

A Coronation dinner is being organised by the Capetown Corporation and Coronation Celebration Committee at the Missions to Seamen Institute for all the crews in Table Bay and in the docks at Capetown on June 26th. The transport fleet will thus not be quite overlooked in the general rejoicings.

The total number of the officers and men of the Royal Navy who are to be brought to London for the Coronation is 3,169, and of the (Royal Marines 3,057. Towards these totals Chatham will contribute 1,032 bluejackets and 762 Marines; Plymouth, 917 blue- jackets and 713 Marines: and Portsmouth, 1,220 bluejackets with six guns and 1,473 Marines, 109 Marines being sent by the depot at Deal.

An organised plan of one vast orderly procession is suggested as the best way of enabling the great crowd to see the decorations along Vie line of the Coronation route.

Active preparations are being made at the London Guildhall for the reception of the King and Queen In the occasion of their visit to the City on July Zrd. The suite of rooms will be splendidly furnished. In the forecourt stands are being erected, right and left, to accommodate about 1,000 invited guests.

Nearly 1,400 artists, three hundred pianists, and a large number of bands have offered their services in connection with the entertainments to be given to the King's poor guests on July sth.

The popular idea that judges assume the black cap preparatory to pronouncing sentence of death in order to mark the tragedy of the occasion should be finally dispelled by the fact that they will wear the same sable headgear at the Coronation. What the coronet is to the peer the black cap is to the judge—the finial of his official costume. The black cap as an essential part of the official costume must be worn on all State occasions, of which the passing sentence of capital punishment is only one.

Some of us are working the Coronation idea a little too hard, writes a correspondent. We are accustomed to Coronation hats and boots, but a friend of mine was rather surprised the other morning to see that her greengrocer had labelled his gooseberries "Coronation berries."

On a prominent shoulder of the Kentish Downs a gigantic crown, visible for miles rour,d, has been etit down to the chalk by the students of the g.auth-Eastern Agricultural College at Wye.

vssa snission which the Pope is sending to attend the Coronation festivitives in London will stay in Paris until the Coronation itself has taken place, in order to avoid attending a religious ceremony in a Protestant church.

Said Ali, who will represent the Sultan of Zanzibar at the Coronation, has arrived in London. Said Ali was educated at Harrow.

The Baroness Burdett-Coutts has arranged through, Mr. John Kirk, the secretary, to present each of the six or seven thousand young cripples on the register of the Ragged School Union Cripples' Mission with a souvenir of the Coronation. Messrs. Doulton and Co. have generously promised to send 6,500 "beakers," similar to the King's gift, to the offices of the Mission. .

SERIOUS ILLNESS Of the King. The Coronation Postponed.

LATEST BULLETINS.

An operation successfully

performed. The illness of the King and the postpone- ment of the Coronation created profound con- sternation in the Metropolis, and indeed all over the country. The rumour was first in circulation directly after noon. The official announcement caused the greatest excitement. No date is mentioned or indicated for the carrying out of the Coronation ceremonies, but it is feared that some weeks, if not months, must elapse before the King will be in a con- dition to undergo the ardous labours connected with the Coronation ceremony.

The first official intimation of tha sad news was as follows : The King is suffering from Perityphytis. The condition on Saturday was so satisfactory that it was hoped that with care His Majesty would be able to go through the Coronation ceremony. On Monday evening a recrudescence became manifest, rendering a surgical operation necemsary to-day. (Signed) LISTER THOMAS SMITH. FRANCIS H. LAKING. TEIoMAS BARLOW. FREDERICK TREVES. June 21th, 11 15 a.m.

THE OPERATION PERFORMED. The following bulletin was posted at the Mansion HousA yesterday afternoon : " The operation on His Majesty has been successfully performed

" A large abscess has been evacuated. "The King has borne the operation well and is la a satisfactory condition." The Press Association adds :—lt is understood that the operation was performed by Sir Frederick Treves, who diagnosed the complaint, and was the specialist called in in consequence of the King's condition. The actual operation was performed at about half-past one, and at a little past two o'clock a Court official informed a representative of the Press Association that the distinguished patient was just recovering satisfactorily from the effects of the at:esthetic.

===== 0002601_19020627_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
Celebrations in the Country. Message from the King.

"The Earl Marshal has received the King's commands to express His Majesty's deep sorrow that owing to his serious illness the Coronation ceremony must be post- poned. All celebrations in London will in consequence be likewise postponed, but it is the King's earnest hope that the celebrations in the country shall be held as already arranged."

6 P.M. BULLETLN. The following bulletin was issued at 6 p.m. : His Majesty continues to make satisfactory progress, and has been much relieved by the operation.—(Signed), Lister, Fredk. Treves, Thomas Smith, Francis H Laken, and Thomas Brrlow.

The news of the poitponement of the Coronation festivities reached Runcorn about three o'clock on Tues- day afternoon. The information was first made known through the proprietors of one or two newspaper shops who conveyed the sad intelligence to the townsfolk by posting the message on their windows. At first the news was discredited but as the afternoon wore on substantial confirmation of the report was re- ceived by public gentlemen who had business relations in London, by means of the telegraph. At the time of writing (Tucsday evening) it is uncertain what will be the action of the !local authorities. The Lord Chamber- lain on behalf of the Bing wishes the country festivities to contince and whether Runcorn in view of this pronouncement will adhere to its official programme is difficult to determine. At pre- I sent the decorations which were proceeding rapidly, have been bi ought to a standstill the majority being undecided whetter to continue or to diamant le what they bad commenced The shopkeepers, too, are placed in a difficult position their action really having to be guided by the action of the Council. The majority of the shops bad determined to remain open on Wednesday, a half holiday, until a late hour and close on Thursday, Friday, and in some cases, Faturday. What they will now do remains to be seen. So far the result is, confusion, dislocation and deadlock.

1 HE PROGRAMME AT RUNCORN.

--- The official programme for Thursday, the 26th of June, states that there will be a procussion of magis- trates, public authorities, friendly societies Sec. At the time of going to press we do not know whether the programme will be carried cut, but in case it is, the place of assembly will be at the Market Place. The proceedings will commence at 9.45 a.m, with a short religions service and the united bands will afterwards play" God save the King." The rrocession will wend its way through the principal streets of the town and return to the Market Place. At 3 p.m. old English sperts are announced to take place on the Football Field. The evening attractions will in- clude a lantern parade and a bonfire on the Beacon Rock, Runcorn Hill. Friday's arrangements are of a very complete character In the morning there will be a demonstration of trades and manufacturers of Rano rn. The procession will be arranged in sections and it will represent the construc- tive, distributive, and productive trades of the town. At two o'clock the procession of Sunday schools will start from the Market-place, under the conductorship of Mr W Handley. The following will be ths ceder of procession (scholars to walk 4 abreast) : The Band of the Weston Wesleyan Sunday School Chairman of the Council Magistrates. Councillors, &a. • Brunswick Wesleyan Methodist Camden Wesleyan Methodist Hope Hail Mission Ashridge-street Mission Welsh Church Salvation Army Halton Road Wesleyan Methodist. Primitive Slethodists—Greenivay Road. St Edward's Romad Catholic St John's Presbyterian Bethesda Congregational dist Nelson-street Wesleyan Metho St. Luke's Congregational Bfiesmere-street United Methodist Free Church Mersey Mission to Seamen St Michael's Church Holy Trinity Mill Brow Mission (Ragged School) Holy Trinity Church Parish Church. Tea will be given to the scholars immediately after the procession. At 630 the Sunday school eche lars w,ll assemble on tne football field to witness sports. At 730 there will be a public dinner of the towns- people of RUBCOM at the Technical Inatittite,to celebrate the return of the Runcorn Volunteers,Ycomen,Reservists and Militiamen from the seat of war in South Africa. Farther celebrations will take place on Wednesday, July 2nd, when a treat to the aged people of the town will be given in the Public Hall at 530 pin, to be followed by an entertainment. Saturday, July 12th will be made memorablA by the laying of the foundation stone of the cottage hospital by Sir John T Brunner, M.P. From the municipal buildinga and the gentlemen's residences to the enterprising tradesman and the humble cot there appears to have been one desire—to make the good old town of Runcorn as pretty as possible in view of the now postponed ceremony. At the Town Hall an energetic band of workers has been busily engaged for some time in the endeavour to make the building , a thing of beauty. Flags have been prettily festooned round the building from long poles on which float the national colours. At Savages' Bridge a wooden structure has been erected which was intended to make an c x eedingly fine triumphant arch. The CO- operative Stores have made a most imposing dis lay. mapincent premises which occupy such a protni- nent position are most tastefully and lavishly decorased and promise to be among the foremost in the town. The unique design of the Gas Company, which is Pr( ceed in High-street, originated by Mr R Frith, manager, is one of the sights which Should prove to be of absorbing interest. All the trades- men have made extensive preparations far an elaborate show and even the side streets are displaying an amount of bunting which exceeds all records. There is not the slightest doubt that Runcorn is not one whit behind any town of its own size in demonstrations of loyalty to the king.

i THE EXAMINER WEDNESDAY JUNE 25, 1902 WARRINGTON FRIENDLY SOCIETIES.

ju anticipation of the Friendly Societies Procession which has now been postponed in consequence of the terious illness of the King we have prepared the following interesting particulars with reference to the Societies and brief biographical sketches of leading men associated with them. We believe they will be found of historic interest to all members of Friendly Societies in Warrington]

and es such represented the Societies on the Mayor's ?iCoronation committee. Bro Maybury has the respect and high opinion of his fellow Foresters. PETER EDGAR, P.P.O S. Shepherdry has claimed Mr Peter Edgar, P.P.C.S. as a member since August 21st, 1875, when he was initiated a member of the Morning Star Lodge of the Warrington district. After he had been a mem- ber twelve months he had a desire to be of some service to the lodge, and undertook the duties of minor offices, which followed in quick succession. He was then called upon ti fulfil the duties of worthy master, and on the retirement of Mr Potter the e,eratarv, in 1879, Mr Edgar was unanimously elected to succeed him. While serving in this capacity he brought about many improvements in the lodge, both in the system of book keeping and other reforms calculated to improve their financial position. The members of the lodge in recogni • lion of his abilities elected him repeatedly the delegate to represent them at the district meetings. In 1883 a further honour V 783 bestowed upon him, for he was elected deputy-chief of the district,and in the following year he was raised to the higher po,d- tion of provincial chief. Whilst serving in this capa- city he was Presented with a handsomely-framed Emblem of Merit. As a further mark of esteem, in the year 1839 he was unanimously chosen to repre- sent the Warrington district at the A M.C., held at Newcastle-under-Lyne, and was also elected on the board of management. In the year 1892 the posi- tion of District Secretary became vacant. A depu- tation waited upon Mr Edgar, and he allowed him- self to be nominated as a candidate for the office, and at the following meeting of the district, he was elected provincial corresponding secretary by a large majority. Mr Edgar is also a trustee of the Juvenile Burial Society in connection with the dis- trict, and examiner of the same fund. From these few details it will be seen that Mr Edgar's connec- tion with local Shepherdry has been a most active and valuable one. But not only has he worked energetically on behalf of this society but his inter- est extends to other institutions. He takes a lively interest in the Friendly Societies Medical Associa- tion, and owing to the numerous friends he has made since he has be7onged to them he was elected president of the Association, and held that position for four years. The treasurership became vacant and he was unanimously appointed to that position, Mr Edgar is also connected with the Free Masons and holds office in connection with that institution. In religion, Mr Edgar is a Churchman, and has held the position of organist and choirmaster at St. Peter's Church for many years.

rington District some eight years ago. He was often requested to take office in his lodge, but owing to his time being fully occupied in pursuing his studies for the examinations of his profession, he was not prevailed upon to allow himself to be elected an officer of the lodge until 1896. He showed marked ability in the fulfilment of the duties appertaining to the various offices,and whilst acting as vice grand he was appointed a delegate to represent his lodge at the district.meet- ing. Never in the history of the Warrington dietrict had a brother holding an office below that of Noble Grand been elected to the district meeting, and some doubt was entertained as to whether Bro Steel was eligible. However when the date for the meeting arrived he took his seat without °opted- tion, and has continued to represent his lodge in various capacities to the credit of himself and with benefit to the district and order. In the year 1897 the occasion of the late Queen's Diamond Jubilee Mr Steel was the Noble Grand of the Constitution Ledge and as such represented it on the C )mmittee whe had charge of the arrangements for inaugurating the Friendly Societies procession wl.ich was held in this town in July of that year, and which was such a decided success. Violist occupying the position of Noble Grand (in 1897) he won the high opinion of his fellow members by his genial temperament, good humour, and the strict impartiality with which he conducted the business of the Lo ige. On the completion of the allotted term, the members acknowledged Bro Steel's services by presenting him with a Past Grand's jewel, the first of its kind ever given to a member of the Consti- tution Lodge, although the lodge had been estab- lished for no less a period than eighty year. The presentation was made by Bro Joan Emson, C.S. of the Warrington District, in the pre- sence of a crowded lodge, every lodge in the town being represented (which spoke volumes for the popularity of the recipient), and many speeches eulogist'c of Mr Steel's services were made. It was not to be wondered at that when Mr Steel's name was submitted in January, 1901, to the district meeting as a candidate for the office of Deputy Grand Master of the District, that he should be at the top of the poll with a good majority. Great pleasure was expressed amongst the members when it was known that Mr Steel would be their Grand Master for the Corona- tion year, and in January last he was unanimously raised to the highest honour his District could con- fer upon him. Though his profession leaves him little spare time, yet the subject of this notice con- tinues to take a lively interest in all that concerns

ance nerd or charter of that lodge, it appears that Bros. A Sixton, Thomas Morris, Thomas Pickavance, and a few others from St. EUlens were the prima movers. The town was stormed by these men, and willing recruits being quickly forthcoming—some of whom afterwards became enthusiastic and earnest workers in thecause— the above lodge was formed. Local enthusiasm ran high, for in the year 1859 the Sons of the Mersey Lodge was opened,and two years afterwards (1861) tho leading lights of the above lodges applied for and obtained the sanction and authority of the Board of Management to form themselves into a separate district, Tnis event gave an ,mpetus to the leaders,for during the next three years four lodges were opmed. Since its inception the District has continued to prosper. In the year 1868— when the district had the honour of having the A.M.C. held in its midst—the number of lodges in the Order had increasod to 1,375, with 85 district, and a membership of 32,767. The leading spirits were, at this time, such men as the late Henry Bruce, James Clare, William Stirrup, Lawrence Gelloolly, Broady, Potter, Travis, and Slider. These brethren along with others, did yeoman service, and it is largely due to their labours that the district has attained to its present position, weile th work which they bean is now being continued with the same earnestness of purpose by the following : Bros La wreece Weir, James Parr, W Eckersley, W Cleave, William T Duckworth, James Lowe, John Haley, A Potter, Thomas Miller, Peter Edgar, F S Massey and others. From a perusal of the last report of the District it appears there are 10 lodges with a mem- herehip of 922 and funds amounting to over £5,000. Mest of the lodges are in a flourishing and healthy con- dition. The District and lodges have just received from the Valuers (Messrs Abbott Brothers, of Sheffield) their Qeniquennial valuation report which is of a most com- plimentary character. Several of the lodges are in such a healthy financial condition that they are in a position to pay more than 299 in the £ per member and if their present favourable condition continues they will be worth much more. The Order generally has had a steady progress year be year. Daring the past year (1901)11436new members were admitted. There are 1036 lodges with 103 districts and a membership, including honorary and juvenile members of nearly 170,000)10th a capital of over £700,000. That it was ono of the earliest lodges connected with the Manchester Unity may be gathered from the fact that it stands 31st in the list of the 4,855 lodges comprising the Order. Biographical Sketches of Leading

Officials.

JOHN MISON, PROV. C.

One who has taken a prominent part in connec- tion with local friendly societies is Mr John Emson, Prov C.S. He has acted as president of the Friendly Societies' Procession Committee and filled the position with credit to himself and the socie- ties. Mr Emson's first connection with a friendly societs began at 19 years of age, when he was working in Manchester, where he joined the Duchess of Devonshire Lodge of the National Independent Order of Oddfell4ws. Having obtained a situation at Messes 'Robert Garnett and Sons, cabinet makers, he left Manchester for W arrington and was transferred to the Garibaldi Lodge in which lodge he still remains. In 1870 he also decided to become a member of the Admiral Nelson Lodge, connected with the Manchester Unity of Odd- fellows. He was soon put in office and mounting the ladder step by step he passed the chairs, after which he was appointed secretary of the lodge, an office which he still holds. He was also lodge secre- tary for some years of a Society in Warrington known as the Childrens' Burial Society. which was formed in March,lB6B, for the benefit uf Oddfellows and which has a capital at the present time of about .E 4,000. The lodge having sent him to represent them at the district meetings,he was several times selected as district auditor and in 1882 he was elected deputy grand master of the district, and in 1883, grand master. On the death of the provincial C.S. (Mr John Houghton) in 1883, Mr Emson was elected to the position of C.S., an office he still holds. Mr Emson was also secretary for many years of the Garibaldi Lodge, N.1.0.F., and district secretary for 15 years of the Warrington district of the National Independent Order of Oddfellows. a branch of the order, flourished in that year. The same year another lodge was opened at the Bull and Mouth Inn, in Sheffi IL In the year 1812-13, a disturb- ance took place in the method of government, and the Nottingham Ancient Imperial Order of Oddfellows and others sprang into being. There is A gap from the year 1812 down to 1837 in the history of the order as there are no records to show what progress WBb made in the interval. In 1837, however, just 65 years ago, there are proofs that Bro. James Sunderland of Sheffield was appointed grand secretary of the order. At the present time it is interesting to note that there are no fewer than 779 lodges in the United Kingdom with a membership of 77,418, whilst throughout the rest of the world there are 3,012 lodges and 191,503 members or a total of 241,771, all told, with a joint capital of £1,067,093. The order has paid in sick and funeral benefits during the last 12 years over 1 millions and over £3,000 in relief to their members when on travel in search of employment. The Warrington district is one of the oldest in the order dating as far back as 1823 and consists of 5 lodges with a membership of over 275, and a capital of over £7BO. There is also a juvenile society in connection with the district with 1,025 mem- , bets and • capital of over £2,050. The juvenile branch lis not confined to members of the order alone, but sny . .

_ person, parents or guardians may enter children int; I the same according to its rules by applying to any of the following oflicir3 :—Samuel Joynson, secretary of the Juvenile Branch ; John Appleton, treasurer of the Juvenile Branch; James Daniels, Chairman of Directors; Thomas Kilroy, deputy-Chairman of Directors, or from the District Master Robert H Gibsoe,and deputy-District .....

Oxley District ireasurer. John Pownall. NATIONAL UNITED ORDER OF FREE GARDENERS.

it is just sixty-five years since the first Warrington lodge in connection with this order was initiated. The lodge in question was known as the Crown sad Dahlia. Since that date considerable progress has been inade,and , the Warrington district now comprises three lodges, two of welch are in Warrington and one in Liverpool. These ' are the Paddington Glory. and Moss Rose in Warrington, snd the Rose of West Derby, Liverpool. The total membership is 193, and the funds amount to about £4OO. The district officers are T J Brookes, D.M., James Rudge, district secretary, and W H Taft, district treasurer. There is a tradition that the original Gardeners of the English Orders came from the north. So many different bodies exist under tbe generic name that it is evident considerable secessions have taken place from the parent body. There are. for instance, such societies as the Order of Free Gardeners in Scot- land ; the Ancient Order of Free Gardeners in the North of England ; the British Order of Free Gardeners; The United Order of Free Gardeners in Shropshire and the

Loyal Order of Free Gardeners. The great bulk of the Gardeners are, however, in the National United Order of Fret, Gardeners which previous to its registration under the kot of 1875 was known as the United Order

of Free Gardeners. It is difficult to assign a date to the establishment of this order It arose is Oldham and neighbourhood probably about the year 1820. Up to 1840 about 500 dispensations to open lodges were issued. In 1841 an agitation commenced in the Yorkshire lodges of the Order with regard to the initiatory ceremonies and lectures, and against the enormous expense attending the system of relief of members when tramping in search of work. They were allowed 23 a day and also what they could receive as gifts from various lodges. Like many other good things this system was abused by men who managed to make a good living out of what was ' intended as a temporary relief. The result of the agita- tation was a secession in 1843 and several of the York- shire lodges formed a new Order and called it " The Order of Ancient Free Gardeners, Yorkshire Union." After a separate existence of nearly thirty years it merged again into the parent society, and the United Order, thus reinforced, went on prosperously. At the end of 1901 there was a membership of 62,153 in addi- tion to 9,244 juveniles, 1,626 widows and female benefit members 898 honorary rnembers,giving a total member- ship of 73,921. The number ot districts is 100, and there are 652 lodges.

ANCIENT ORDER OF FORESTERS. In order to trace the histcry of the Ancient Order of Foresters in Warrington we shall have to go back to the early days of the present century. The Order of Foresters was the outcome of a disruption of the Royal Foresters who were in existence in the year 1745, of which order the oldest court in the Warrington dis- trict was a branch, :limber 138, The disruption took place in the year 1833, when the court already men- boned along with Manchester and several other districts seceded, and formed the Ancient Order of Foresters. In the year 1841, the seventh high court of the Order was held in Warrington at which 244 delegates were present. The Warrington District was formed at this time, the first district secretary being Mr John E Tilling who held the office for about three years. Several members of his family are still in the order. Ile was succeeded by Mr ne—nan f,nwe who lialei the nflice for sixteen care. and during that period, Xnutstora ...,aed from the district and formed one of its own. Mr James Lowe was the next district secretary and he held tte office for 29 years. During his period of office, Hollins Green seceded from the Warrington district and formed a separate district. It was also whilst Mr James Lowe was district secre- tary that a Widow and Orphans' society was formed that the second degree of the known as the Ancient Order of Shepherds, was granted ; and that a Juvenile and Adult Burial Society was formed. Upon the proposal of Mr Lowe, privilege was granted to mem- bers from other towns to pay their contributions in the own where they were located, and from that rne the district has increased numerically and financially. The present district secretary, Mr. L Davies has worked energetically on behalf of the Society,and the distriot now stands as follows : Number of members, 1,134, comprising 6 courts ; funeral fund, £1,850; sick fund, £4,050 ; management and subsidiary fund, £250 ; widowa and orphans fund and second degree of Foresters, 130 members • capital, £520; juvenile and adults burial society, 1,690 members, capital, £2,650 ; makir,g a grand total of £9,320 for the VVarrington district. The Order, taken as a whole, com- prises 928,035 male and female members ; a net increase of 13,452 during the veer 1901. The funds of the Order on December 31st, 1901, amounted to £7,172,833.

[NDEPENDENT ORDER OF RECHABITES, WAR- RINGTON DISTRICT, No 4.

This district was instituted in December, 1836. and registered May, 1889. The area of the district extends from Stockton Heath to Orrell on the other side of Wigan, and is composed of sixteen tents, of which War- rington has four, namely : Cearity No. 11 ; 156 mem- bers ; Purity. No. 2,853, 32 members ; Prosperity (San- key Bridges, 21 members. There are also tents at Latch- ford. P-dgate, Stockton Heath, Penketla. Earlestown, Lowton, dome Green, Garswood, Golao:ne. Bunn and Orrell. The total membership is 1,124. The districtOhas a funeral fund of £1,439 3s 9d, and a sick fund of £1,882 is; total, £3,321 4; Id. For many years the district stood at a very low ebb,but since 1889 when it was registered it began to stir itself, and rose from a membership of 250 in 1888 to 595 in 1897, and 1.124 ;n 1902. The Charity Tent has always met in Friar's Green Chapel. Cairo-street, and up to 1891 had only a membership of 23. At that date the officers determined to see if this state of things could not be altered, and they therefore put their shoulders to the wheal with the result that the tent now has a member- ship of 156,principally young members,and also a strong juvenile tent with over 200 members. and is in a good financial position. The Union tent has met in several schoolrooms in the town. It is now located in the Temperance Mission Room, Academy-;treet. It has re. mained fairly steady in numbers vlrying betw,.en 60 and 70 for a number of years: It now number 69, and has a sick fund amounting to over £6OO, and also a Small Juvenile Tent. Th's tent numbers amongst its members several veterans who have been for over 50 veari; 'lnhabits; in this tent. The Rechabite Order has a member-bip of over 275.000 adult and juvenile mem- bers and over £1,250,000 in funds.

WARRINGTON DISTRICT SOCIETY OF DRUIDS. It was in the year 1836 that the Warrington District Society of Druids was established with 12 members who formed themselves into a lodge called the Rosebud Lodge which was affiliated to the then Order or Society called the Modern Druids. The remarkable advancement and success which attended their efforts is exemplified by the establishment in the following year (the year of her late Majesty's accession to the throne)oiltwo new lodges with an additional memb,rship of 27, thus bringing up their total memirrship to 39, with funds amounting to nearly £IOO. During the next two years, two more new lodges were (maned and at the end of the year 1839, I they had 100 members on their five lodge books. I Each year afterwards found it extending its influence and usefulness, opening up new ground whenever oppor- tunities presented themselves, adding to their numbers and increeing their capital. The Druids now number 13 lodges with a membership of 1100 male members and 562 female members, and 1900, children or juvenile members making a grand total of 3,562 members, with a total of all funds in the district of £7,250. Besides attending to many duties required of the-n for their larder, the Druids have the credg of being the pioneers of the establishment of the Medical Association in the town, which is now largely supported by members of other orders. All the Druids in the district are members and supporters of this institution, whicia has been a great !access end conferred a great boon on Friendly Societies' members, as they now have a medical man and dispenser to attend to their wants in this restiect. The district was specially favoured in the year 1877, and the town honoured by tbe annual delegation of the Order of Druids b?ing held here in Whit-week, at which some 69 representatives from all parts of the country attended. For the past seven years the Society has been carrying on its work in the Druids' Flall, Sankey-greet. B )sides the district body assembling here, many of the lodgee connected with it hold their meetings at the hall.

WARRINGTON A Fireman Found Drowned at Warrington. On Saturday, at noon, Police Constable Hardy received information that a body was floating in the Bridgewater Canal, near Black sear Bridge, War- rington. With the assistance of Police Constable Monks the body was recovered and removed to the mortuary, where it was identified on Monday as that of James Edward Black, aged twenty-ene, s fireman, of Gateshead-on-Tyne. H. M. ASHTON'S LIFE TONIC is Tait KING OF TONICS.-63. SANKEY-STREET, Warrington.—(Advt). Put his Fist Through the Window. John Brophy, a stranger, was charged at the County Borough Police Court on Monday, with having been drunk in Peter-street on Sanday after- noon, and -with having wilfully broken a window in the restaurant of Mrs Dean. From the evidence of a boy named John Doherty it appeared that prisoner knocked at the door of the restaurant, and receiving no answer put his fist through the win- dow. He was fined 5s and costs and ordered to pay ~£1 2s for the damage.

MOTOR BICYCLES.—The " Bayliss Thomas Exf"9 sior "Sole Agent. J a Garmaxn,Bridge-Bt, Warrington Success of Local Bands. A musical festival, with which was combined sports, was held in Hawarden Park on Saturday , the proceeds being devoted to paying for the new instruments of the local Volunteer Band. The pro- gramme consisted of brass band contests,male voice choir competitions, and sports, In the competition for the quickstep, which was played from the station to the pa*, the Earlestown Viaduct Band was awarded first prize. In the competition in playing the test piece " Songs of Other Days," the Warrington Borough Band secured the first prize, and the Earlestown Viaduct Band the fifth prize. Ten bands competed.

COUNTY OF CHESTER, TO WIT. NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that the next GENERAL QUARTER SESSIONS of the Peace for the COUNTY OF CHESTER will be holden at the CASTLE OF CHESTER, in and for the said County en MONDAY, the 30th day of June next, at half-past Eleven o'clock in the forenoon, when the Grand Jury will be empannelled. The hearing of 311 Appeals arising within the Hundreds of Broxton, Eddiehury, Nantwich, and Wirral, forming the Chester District, and the trials of all prisoners com- mitted from such Hundreds, will take place at such Sessions. The Grand and Petty Juries for the Chester District must be in attendance at the Castle of Chester. at the opening of the Cunt on MONDAY, the 30th day of June Next, at half-past Eleven o'clock in the Fore- noon. THE Court will be Adjourned to the Court House, Knutsford, on WEDNESDAY, the 2nd day of July next, at Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon, when another Grand Jury will be empannelled. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Hundreds of Bucklow, Macclesfield, and Northwich, forming the Knutsford District, and the trials of all prisoners committed from the last-mentioned Hundreds will take place at such Adjourned Sessions The Grand and Petty Juries for the Knutsford District must be in attendance at the Court House, Knutsford,at the opening of the Court on WEDNESDAY, the 2nd day of July next, at Eleven o'clock in the Foreno3n. REGINALD POTTS, Clerk of the Peace for the County of Chester. Chester, 9th June, 1902.

piE RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL OF RUNCORN. HELSBY SEWERAGE. The Rural District Council of Runcorn Invite Tenders for the construction of new Sewerage and sewage Disposal Works at Helaby. The works will comprise about 2 miles of Bin to 121 n Stoneware Pipe sewers. together with all manholes, lamp- shafts, flushing arrangements and other appurtenances, and 560 yards of 61n Iron Pumping Main ; the construction of Pumping Station and Storage Tank and Concrete Filter Beds and laying out small land Filtration Area. Tho Plans may be seen and Quantities and Forms of Tender obtained at the office of the Engineer, Mr. W. H. Radford 0.E., Albion Chambers, Nottingham, on deposit of Two Guineas, which will be returned on receipt of a bona- fide tender and the return of the quantities. The plans may also be seen at the house of Mr. William Diggle, the Council's Surveyor, Holly Bank, Frodsham. Sealed tenders endorsed " Tender for Helsby Sewerage Works "to be sent in to me not later than the 12th day of July, 1902. Ihe Council do not bind themselves to accept the lowest or any tender. THE CORONATION. (26th June, 1902.) How shall we crown the King' With a great diadem of gems and gold, Red rubies, sapphires bluer than the sky, Rich emeralds, and diamonds manifold, Pearls, milky opals, lapis lazuli' They might be proud to cling About the brows of Alfred's latest heir, The royal offspring of a thousand years, Victoria's dazzling sceptre born to bear. Whose shining memory all the world reveres, How shall we crown the King' His Queen beside him, flower of all her line, Daughter of sea-kings from across the sea, Already crowned by Love's own "right divine," And mother of England's mightier Kings to be; With silver trumpeting, Epon the stone which kings on kings have graced, Amid the dust where kings on kings lie dead, While [Fe great organ's golden voles is raised, And golden sunlight on the Throne is shed? Mow shall we erown the King' With the wild outburst of a nation's love, Articulate new in every teeming street Where the lest-hearted millions slowly move. And the world-city's pulse is all abeat, The least lip fain to sing ; With revel and feast In every surging town That lifts its windows to the morning sun, And every smiling village and hamlet, erown The King we look to as the kingly one? How shall we crown the King? With tribute rare across the ocean borne From rising realms, and races "ot the blood," Ammo rich continents of ripening corn, And mighty commonwealths who share our mood; From dusky tribes who bring Warm hearts beneath black bosoms, arms as strong As the young lions that in their jungles borne ; With label greetings of a greater throng That ever gathered in the streets of Rome? How shall we crown the King? With thunderous salute of tattle shins That move in silence round our English world, Following the sun for !over as he dips. To find for ever the ame flag unfurled ; With bells whose changes ring, And rook, and roar, from steeples fronting heaven In every clime where English feet have trod In every zone to his dominion given, And every nation dear to him and God? How shall we crown% ? With the white lilies of enduring peace. Bought with the heart's blood of our bravest sons In quivering continents where every breeze, Throbbed with the thunder of our English guns ? With the two realms who fling Their rides from them, having learnt, at last, That, losing freedom, they may still be free And share the birthright of an empire vaat That broadens into world wide sovereignty ? How shall we crown the King? With roar .of rockets, and beacons bloody red, With clamorous cities from end to end afire, And Ilaaeeeeetlo tattles with great banquets spread : With Zeanty's smiles, with Love. and with Desire ; With all the flowers of spring, And all the fabrics woven In looms untold ; With glittering precessions of priests and peers ; With glory of green and purple, of crimson and gold, And the unending tempest of men's cheers P These will not crown the King ? No blaze of jewels, or shimmer of purple nag geld, No pomp of music, end flutter of flags unfurled. No thunder of clamorous acclamation rolled Hound the four corners of the listening world ; Not all the bells that swing, Nor all the bishops who ever donned a stole, Can make the unkindly a king, the kingly less . " Self-reverence, sell-knowledge, sell-control," Self-sacrifice, and splendid seldessnets, O RulerrTlT flock okee creoeki infrequent t :fro oreeeTangqopli swayed, Take all ewinhoorsetritkreultyhelosytartintgha. ; With timorous hand upon thy footstool laid Aaynbbfkaeolytnegerrslatnyge,otnOgng:le, n I Aid, therefore, on thy Coronation Day, I also greet thee, and make bold to cry : The King must crown the King I ARTHUR BENNETT.
